Hopf algebras of parking functions and decorated planar trees Thanks: Bergeron is partially supported by NSERC and the York Research Chair in applied algebra. Thanks: Pang and Li are partially supported by the Hong Kong Research Grants Council grant ECS 22300017. Thanks: This work started at the Algebraic Combinatorics workshop at the Fields institute.
Nantel Bergeron, Rafael S. González D’León, Shu Xiao Li, C. Y. Amy Pang, Yannic Vargas

Abstract
. We construct three new combinatorial Hopf algebras based on the Loday-Ronco operations on planar binary trees. The first and second algebras are defined on planar trees and labeled planar trees extending the Loday-Ronco and Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebras respectively. We show that the latter is bidendriform which implies that is also free, cofree, and self-dual. The third algebra involves a new visualization of parking functions as decorated binary trees; it is also bidendriform, free, cofree, and self-dual, and therefore abstractly isomorphic to the algebra 𝖯𝖰𝖲𝗒𝗆 \operatorname{\mathsf{PQSym}} of Novelli and Thibon. We define partial orders on the objects indexing each of these three Hopf algebras, one of which, when restricting to ( m + 1 ) (m+1) -ary trees, coarsens the m m -Tamari order of Bergeron and Préville-Ratelle. We show that multiplication of dual fundamental basis elements are given by intervals in each of these orders. Finally, we use an axiomatized version of the techniques of Aguiar and Sottile on the Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra to define a monomial basis on each of our Hopf algebras, and to show that comultiplication is cofree on the monomial elements. This in particular, implies the cofreeness of the Hopf algebra on planar trees. We also find explicit positive formulas for the multiplication on monomial basis and a cancellation-free and grouping-free formula for the antipode of monomial elements.



1.
Introduction

The algebraic structure of a combinatorial Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{H},\cdot,\Delta)
can often be understood in terms of a poset on the underlying family of combinatorial objects indexing a basis. For instance, in the Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra of permutations
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}

[
MR95
]
, the product of fundamental basis elements, and of dual-fundamental basis elements, are sums over an interval in the right and left weak orders, respectively.
Another example is the multiplication in the Loday-Ronco Hopf algebra of binary trees
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}^{*}

[
LR98
]
which is the sum of an interval in the Tamari order.
Furthermore, the relationship between these two particular algebras is encoded through poset morphisms going both ways, from permutations to binary trees and vice versa. In
[
AS05
]
these morphisms are shown to form a Galois connection between the two posets. These two morphisms also reveal a classical connection between these two families of combinatorial objects: the set of permutations of
[n]:=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is in bijection with the set of increasing binary trees with
n
internal nodes, which are planar rooted binary trees whose set of internal nodes are decorated with a bijection to
[n]
in a way that any path away from the root has an increasing sequence of labels. A consequence of this interpretation of permutations as labeled planar binary trees is that forgetting the labels induces a map between permutations and planar binary trees. In Figure
1
we illustrate the
6
permutations of
[3]
in one-line notation besides their corresponding increasing binary tree.

321

Figure 1.
Permutations of
[3]
.

1.1.
New combinatorial Hopf algebras on trees

Inspired on the relationship between
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
, we study three new combinatorial Hopf algebras supported on the set
PT
of planar rooted trees and
LPT
of labeled planar rooted trees. The definition of the multiplication and comultiplication on the fundamental basis of these algebras are generalizations of the classical operations of “lightening splitting” and “grafting” defined on binary trees (see Figure
2
).

Figure 2.
Splitting by lightening at the leaf
i+1
with
i=2

The first two combinatorial Hopf algebras extend directly
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
to include all planar rooted trees. The fundamental basis in the former Hopf algebra is indexed on
PT
extending that of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
. There are however two different natural extensions of the Hopf structure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
to
PT
depending on whether we permit any splitting by lightening at any leaf
i+1
(which we will denote
t\mapsto({}^{i}t,t^{i})
) or only some splittings which we call “allowable”. Both constructions preserve the number of leaves but the allowable splitting is defined so that the number of internal nodes is also preserved.

Theorem 1.1

(Proposition
4.4
)
.

The two Hopf algebra structures on
PT
induced by the operation of splitting by lightening are isomorphic.

Remark 1.2
.

Theorem
1.1
illustrates a common theme in this area: multiple (a priori) different constructions on the same set of combinatorial objects could lead to isomorphic Hopf algebras. From an algebraic combinatorics point of view, however, the chosen basis and construction is often relevant in the context where these Hopf algebras are being used.

We call
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
the Hopf algebra on
PT
together with the multiplication and comultiplication defined by allowable splitting by lightning.

Our extension of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
to the context of planar trees comes from the concept of the Stirling permutations introduced by Gessel and Stanley in
[
GS78
]
. A (generalized) Stirling permutation can be viewed as a labeled planar tree whose labels are increasing in any path away from the root (see Figure
3
). In particular, for planar trees whose internal nodes all have
3
children, the generalized Stirling permutations are exactly the Stirling permutations defined in
[
GS78
]
. They can also be regarded as
212
avoiding packed words (as defined in
[
J99
]
).

1
1
2
4
3
3
241331

Figure 3.
Generalized Stirling permutation and its underlying planar tree

We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
as the Hopf algebra whose fundamental basis is indexed on the set
GSP
of generalized Stirling permutation and where its multiplication and comultiplication are defined by allowable splitting of the underlying planar tree.

A Hopf algebra
(\mathcal{H},\cdot,\Delta)
has a
bidendriform
structure if it can be provided with operations
\ll
,
\gg
,
\Delta_{\ll}
, and
\Delta_{\gg}
satisfying
\cdot=\ll+\gg
,
\Delta=\Delta_{\ll}+\Delta_{\gg}
and a set of relations described in Section
5.4
. One of the important consequences for a Hopf algebra to be bidendriform is given by Foissy in
[
F07
,
F12
]
where he proves that such an algebra is free, cofree and self-dual. Using this result we prove the following theorem.

Theorem 1.3

(Proposition
5.14
and Corollary
5.15
)
.

The Hopf algebra
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
has a bidendriform structure and hence is free, cofree and self-dual.

Bergeron and Zabrocki show in
[
BZ05
]
that the Hopf algebra
\operatorname{\mathsf{NCQSym}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}^{*}
of noncommutative quasisymmetric functions, supported on packed words, is free and cofree. With the interpretation of Stirling permtuations as packed words we give an embedding
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}^{*}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{NCQSym}}=\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}^{*}
.

We introduce yet a third new combinatorial Hopf algebra on labeled planar trees, the Hopf algebra of parking functions
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
, in which a parking function is viewed as a pair
(\sigma,t)\in\text{Perm}_{n}\times\text{PBT}_{n}
of a binary tree and a permutation of the same degree such that
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)
(see Section
2
for the definitions of these statistics). A consequence of our construction is the existence of natural Hopf algebra epimorphisms to
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
(see Figure
4
).

Theorem 1.4

(Proposition
6.7
and Corollary
6.8
)
.

The Hopf algebra
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
is a bidendriform bialgebra, and hence is free, cofree, self-dual, and isomorphic to the Hopf algebra
\operatorname{\mathsf{PQSym}}
defined in
[
NT07
]
.

It is worthy to mention that, though we do not prove it here, our construction of
\operatorname{\mathsf{PQSym}}
generalizes to the context of
m
-parking functions by using
m+1
-ary trees. This construction is likely related to the constructions in
[
NT20
]
.

The following diagram summarizes the relationships between our three new algebras and other Hopf algebras. Note that the square commutes but the triangle on the left does not.

\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
\Pi
\Pi_{\text{ptree}}
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
\Pi_{\text{perm}}
\Pi_{\text{tree}}
\not\equiv
\equiv
\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}

Relations between the Hopf algebras discussed in this work

1.2.
Underlying posets and poset maps

We define partial orders naturally associated with the three families of combinatorial objects that index the Hopf algebra constructions discussed above.

We define a Tamari order on the set
PT
of all planar trees that when restricting to any family of planar
(m+1)
-ary trees for a fixed
m
coarsens the
m
-Tamari order defined in
[
BP12
]
and when
m=1
it coincides with the classical definition of the Tamari lattice. In the case of labeled planar trees, the set
GSP
of Stirling permutations inherits a planar weak order from the left weak order on packed words
[
V15
]
. Much in the same flavor as in
[
AS05
]
we show that the maps
\pi_{ptree}
leaving the underlying planar unlabeled tree asociated to a Stirling permutation and a particular section
\iota_{gsp}
associating to a planar tree its unique
213
-avoiding Stirling permutation are compatible as stated in the following proposition.

Proposition 1.5

(Corollary
5.27
)
.

The maps
\pi_{ptree}
and
\iota_{gsp}
form a Galois connection between the weak order on Stirling permutations and the Tamari order on planar trees.

Via the identification of a parking function as a pair
(\sigma,t)\in\text{Perm}_{n}\times\text{PBT}_{n}
, parking functions get a natural partial order inherited from the product lattice on the weak and Tamari orders, which we call the parking order.

The definition of partial orders associated to the Hopf algebras
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}^{*}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}^{*}
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}^{*}
allow us to see multiplication in the dual bases as sums over intervals in these posets.

Theorem 1.6

(Propositions
4.8
,
5.28
, and
6.11
)
.

Let
F_{f}^{*}
,
F_{g}^{*}
be elements in the dual basis in any of the graded duals
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}^{*}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}^{*}
, or
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}^{*}
. Then,

m(F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*})=\sum_{f\backslash g\leq h\leq f/g}F_{h}^{*},

where
\leq
are the planar Tamari, weak and monomial partial orders respectively; and
f/g
and
f\backslash g
are obtained by grafting
f
and
g
according to Definitions
2.5
and
2.9
.

1.3.
Monomial basis and axioms

One way to show that a combinatorial Hopf algebra is free is by constructing an explicit basis with a free multiplication
[
LR02
,
BZ05
,
NT06
,
NT07
,
PP18
]
, often of the form

(1)

H_{f}=\sum_{g\leq f}G_{g}

where
G
is the starting basis, and
\leq
is a partial order on the underlying objects. However, besides
[
BZ05
]
, most of the other works do not explore the comultiplication properties of their free
H
bases – are the comultiplication coefficients even non-negative? – so it is not easy to use the new
H
basis to define Hopf morphisms to other Hopf algebras. Furthermore, despite the common formulas of the type in (
1
), in all these algebras there is no universal result from which these cases may be recovered as easy corollaries.

Aguiar-Sottile
[
AS05
,
AS06
]
took with
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
(dual to the Loday-Ronco algebra) and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
an alternative direction resulting in a more complete approach. They make the dual construction to (
1
): if
F
is the starting “fundamental” basis (dual to
G
above), define the “monomial”
M
basis by

(2)

M_{f}=\sum_{g\geq f}\mu(f,g)F_{g},\quad\text{ equivalently }\quad F_{f}=\sum_{g\geq f}M_{g}.

They then show that the monomial basis is cofree, and that the primitive monomial basis elements are indexed by permutations or binary trees with no “global descent”. Playing with Möbius inversion on the poset structure, one then obtains a positive multiplication formula and a cancellation-free antipode formula for the monomial basis. An extension of these techniques to packed words is key to Vargas’ self-duality isomorphism for
\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}

[
V20
]
- it was possible to compare monomial bases on
\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{NCQSym}}\cong\operatorname{\mathsf{WQSym}}^{*}
thanks to the explicit multiplication formula.
These techniques were used also in
[
CS08
,
S20
]

In this article we abstract the technique of Aguiar and Sottile by giving a set of axioms (see Section
3
) based on the interaction between an underlying poset on the set of combinatorial objects indexing the fundamental basis and the Hopf operations of a combinatorial Hopf algebra defined on the same family of objects. These axioms guarantee that the monomial basis defined via (
2
) is cofree. We make abstract definitions of “shifted concatenation” and “global descents” to give a unifying positive multiplication formula and a cancellation-free antipode formula where all terms have the same sign. Our axioms are easily transfered via poset morphisms, meaning that monomial bases may be defined for many combinatorial Hopf algebras on related objects, all in one sweep.

We show that
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
satisfy the axioms in Section
3
. We show this directly on
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
, and we use a transfer axiom to show that from the calculation in
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
we can conclude that the axioms are also satisfied in
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
. As a consequence of these axioms, we obtain explicit formulas for the multiplication and comultiplication of monomial basis elements, and a cancellation-free grouping-free formula for their antipode. We have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.7

(Propositions
4.11
–
4.13
,
5.34
–
5.36
,
6.14
–
6.16
)
.

For the monomial bases
\{M_{t}\}
of the combinatorial Hopf algebras
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
defined as in equation (
2
) we have that

(1)

The comultiplication of monomial
basis elements is given by

\Delta_{+}(M_{t})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}M_{{}^{i}t}\otimes M_{t^{i}}.

(2)

The product of monomial basis elements has non-negative integer coefficients.

(3)

In the antipode image
\mathcal{S}(M_{t})
, all
terms have the same sign, equal to the parity of
|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)|+1
.

As a consequence of Theorem
1.7
we obtain that
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
is cofree. An interesting open question is to determine if
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
is also free and self-dual.

Remark 1.8
.

Restricting
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
to the generating subset of planar
(m+1)
-ary trees for a fixed positive integer
m
we obtain a subalgebra of
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
indexed by Fuss-Catalan objects that is likely isomorphic to the Hopf algebra defined in
[
NT20
]
.

Remark 1.9
.

Note that parts (1) and (2) of Theorem
1.7
require independent axioms, so for a general Hopf algebra one can have (1) without having (2), as it is the case of the tableaux algebra studied in
[
MR21
]
.

The organization of this article is as follows. In section
2
, we review the notation and background on the classical combinatorial Hopf algebras
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
. In section
3
, we present a list of our monomial basis axioms and its consequences without proofs. Sections
4
,
5
and
6
introduce respectively the Hopf algebras of planar trees, generalized Stirling permutations, and parking functions, detailing their algebraic properties and connections with the classical Hopf algebras
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
. Lastly in section
7
, we provide the proofs for the theorems in section
3
.

2.
Background

2.1.
Notations

In this section, we define a number of operations on planar trees and labeled planar trees. These operations also apply to binary trees and labeled binary trees as special cases.

Definition 2.1
.

A (rooted planar)
tree
is a rooted tree such that for each node its set of children is totally ordered and each node either has no child, i.e. a
leaf
, or have at least 2 children i.e. an
internal node
. By convention, there is an
empty tree
with no internal node and
1
leaf, we draw it as “
”.
The
i
-th leaf of
t
reading from left to right is called the
i
-th leaf of
t
for short. The set of planar trees is denoted by
PT
. The
degree
of a tree
t
is one less than number of leaves in
t
, denoted by
\deg(t)
. The
internal degree
of
t
is number of internal nodes of
t
, denoted by
\text{ideg}(t)
. (See for example Figure
5
)

Figure 5.
Planar trees with 4 leaves, i.e. with degree 3.
The trees in row
i
have
i
internal nodes.

Definition 2.2
.

A
labeled (planar) tree
is a tree
f
together with a map
\kappa_{f}:\{\text{internal nodes of }f\}\to\mathbb{N}
that assigns a natural number to each internal node of
f
. The set of labeled trees is denoted by
LPT
. By abuse of notation, we use the symbol
f
for both labeled tree and its underlying tree. If
\kappa_{f}
is an injection, then we may define the
standardization
of
f
, denoted by
\operatorname{\mathtt{std}}(f)
, to be the labeled tree whose underlying tree is the same as
f
and the labels are renumbered to
\{1,2,\dots,\text{ideg}(f)\}
while keeping the relative order.

In this paper, we view permutations, parking functions and increasing trees as labeled trees. We use the symbols
s,t,r
for trees,
\sigma,\tau,\rho
for permutations,
f,g,h
for parking functions and
u,v,w
for generalized Stirling permutations.

We describe below some operations on trees, that will be used to define the comultiplication and multiplication of the associated Hopf algebras.

An orange lightening hits the tree
t
at a leaf, and goes along the path from that leaf to the root. This lightening splits the tree into two pieces, and by contracting unnecessary nodes, we obtain an ordered pair of two trees
(t_{1},t_{2})
. We denote this by
t\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2})
if
(t_{1},t_{2})
, and call it a
deconcatenation
of
t
. When it is useful to specify that the lightening is at the
(i+1)
-th leaf, so
\deg t_{1}=i
, we may write
({}^{i}t,t^{i})
in place of
(t_{1},t_{2})
.

.

. To calculate
{}^{2}t
and
t^{2}
, consider a lightening hitting at the third leaf:

\quad\mapsto\quad

,

,

)
.

Similarly,
k-1
lightenings hitting a tree
t
split it into
k
trees, such process is denoted by
t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
. Multiple lightenings may hit the same leaf, thus
(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is determined by a
weak composition
of
\deg(t)+k
with
k
parts i.e. a sequence of nonnegative integers
(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{k})
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}=\deg(t)+k
.

Example 2.4
.

\hbox to100.39pt{\vbox to72.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 50.19232pt\lower-7.51318pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

We say
t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
is
allowable
if
\text{ideg}(t)=\text{ideg}(t_{1})+\cdots+\text{ideg}(t_{k})
. The splitting in example
2.3
is not allowable while the one in example
2.4
is. Note that if
t
is a binary tree, then all
t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
are allowable.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
s
and
t
be two trees, we define two operations
/
and
\backslash
as follows

•

s/t
is the tree obtained by identifying the root of
s
with the left-most leaf of
t
.

•

s\backslash t
is the tree obtained by identifying the root of
t
with the right-most leaf of
s
.

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.87 7.87 M 9.26 7.87 C 9.26 8.64 8.64 9.26 7.87 9.26 C 7.11 9.26 6.49 8.64 6.49 7.87 C 6.49 7.11 7.11 6.49 7.87 6.49 C 8.64 6.49 9.26 7.11 9.26 7.87 Z M 7.87 7.87}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
. Then

s/t=
and

s\backslash t=
.

It is easy to see that both the above operations are associative. Note also that, if
\deg s=i
, then
{}^{i}(s/t)={}^{i}(s\backslash t)=s
and
(s/t)^{i}=(s\backslash t)^{i}=t
.

Let
s
be a tree with
k
leaves, and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
be
k
trees, we define
s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
to be the tree obtained by identifying the root of
t_{i}
with the
i
-th leaf of
s
for all
i
.

We now define similar operations on labeled trees.

Let
f
be a labeled tree and
f\mapsto(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
be allowable. Note that when the splitting is allowable, each internal node of
f
exists in exactly one
f_{i}
i.e. it does not exist or gets contracted in all other parts. Therefore,
\kappa_{f}
induces a labeling on each
f_{i}
, namely
\kappa_{f_{i}}(v)=\kappa_{f}(v)
. By abuse of notation, such operation is denoted by
f\mapsto(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
where
f_{i}
are labeled trees.

Let
f
be a labeled tree such that
\kappa_{f}
is a bijection between its internal nodes and
\{1,\dots,\text{ideg}(f)\}
. Assume
f\mapsto(f_{1},f_{2})
is allowable with
\deg(f_{1})=i
. We will use a notation where the degree of the split is specified and denote the standardization of
f_{1}
by
{}^{i}f
and the standardization of
f_{2}
by
f^{i}
, i.e.
{}^{i}f=\operatorname{\mathtt{std}}(f_{1})
and
f^{i}=\operatorname{\mathtt{std}}(f_{2})
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
f
and
g
be labeled trees, we define two operations
/
and
\backslash
as follows

•

f/g
is the labeled tree obtained by first shifting labels of
f
up by
\text{ideg}(g)
, and then identifying the root of
f
with the left-most leaf of
g
.

•

f\backslash g
is the labeled tree obtained by first shifting labels of
g
up by
\text{ideg}(f)
, and then identifying the root of
g
with the right-most leaf of
f
.

. Then

f/g=
1
2
3
4
and

f\backslash g=
1
2
3
4
.

It is easy to see that both the above operations are associative. As for unlabeled trees, we again have that, if
\deg f=i
, then
{}^{i}(f/g)={}^{i}(f\backslash g)=f
and
(f/g)^{i}=(f\backslash g)^{i}=g
.

Let
f
be a labeled tree with
k
leaves, and
g_{1},\dots,g_{k}
be
k
labeled trees, we define
f\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
to be the labeled tree obtained by first shifting the labels of each
g_{i}
up by
\text{ideg}(f)
, and then identifying the root of
g_{i}
with the
i
-th leaf of
f
for all
i
.

Consider the map
\pi:\text{LPT}\to\text{PT}
by forgetting the node labels. It is easy to see that
\pi
preserves the operations we defined above.

Proposition 2.12
.

The map
\pi
satisfies the following properties:

(1)

{}^{i}(\pi f)=\pi\left({}^{i}f\right)
,
(\pi f)^{i}=\pi\left(f^{i}\right)
;

(2)

\pi(g/h)=(\pi g)/(\pi h)
;

(3)

\pi(g\backslash h)=(\pi g)\backslash(\pi h)
;

(4)

\pi(f\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(g_{1},\dots,g_{k}))=(\pi f)\shuffle(\pi g_{1},\dots,\pi g_{k})
.

2.2.
Binary trees and the Tamari order

Definition 2.13
.

A (rooted planar)
binary tree
is a tree where each node has either
0
or
2
children, the left child and the right child. The set of planar binary trees is denoted by
PBT
. Let
t
be a binary tree of degree
n
, we define its
descent
set to be
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)=\{1\leq i\leq n-1:\text{the }(i+1)\text{-th leaf of }t\text{ is a right child}\}
.

Example 2.14
.

Binary trees with
3
internal nodes.

Their descent sets are
\emptyset,\{2\},\{1\},\{2\},\{1,2\}
respectively.

A left rotation at node
x
is the following operation, where
y
is the right child of
x
, and
A,B,C
are branches.

x
y
A
B
C
\mapsto
x
y
A
B
C

Definition 2.15
.

The
Tamari order
,
\leq_{T}
, is a partial order on PBT whose covering relations are left rotations. (see for example Figure
6
)

Remark 2.16
.

In the literature, trees are sometimes drawn downwards. Some people use the dual poset as the Tamari poset, depending on reflecting or rotating the tree upwards. Our present convention is the dual of the Tamari order used in
[
AS06
]
.

Figure 6.
The Tamari order on binary trees of degree
3
and
4
.

2.3.
Hopf algebra of binary trees

In this section, we recall the Loday-Ronco Hopf algebra of binary trees
[
LR98
,
AS06
]
.

Definition 2.17
.

As a graded vector space, (the dual of) the Loday-Ronco Hopf algebra,
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
is
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Bbbk
-
\text{PBT}_{n}
where
\text{PBT}_{n}
is the set of binary trees with
n+1
leaves. The fundamental basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
is
\{F_{t}:t\in\text{PBT}\}
.

The comultiplication is by deconcatenation

\Delta(F_{t})=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2})}F_{t_{1}}\otimes F_{t_{2}}=\sum_{i=0}^{\deg f}F_{{}^{i}t}\otimes F_{t^{i}}.

In what follows, let
\Delta_{+}
be defined by
\Delta=Id\otimes 1+1\otimes Id\ +\ \Delta_{+}
.

.

It is not hard to check that this comultiplication is coassociative.

To compute the product
F_{s}\cdot F_{t}
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
, first deconcatenate
t
into
\deg(s)+1
pieces, then attach them to the leaves of
s
:

F_{s}\cdot F_{t}=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})}F_{s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})}.

Example 2.19
.

Let
s=\hbox to23.56pt{\vbox to17.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.78091pt\lower-6.09045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -15.75 15.75}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Remark 2.20
.

Note that our present multiplication is in the opposite order of that from
[
AS06
]
.

It is known that the dual multiplication can be described using intervals on the Tamari order. (This looks different from the formula in

[
LR02
]
because we took the dual of their convention for the Tamari order.)

Proposition 2.21
.

[
LR02
]

Let
s
and
t
be two binary trees, and let
F_{s}^{*}
,
F_{t}^{*}
be their dual basis element in the graded dual
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}^{*}
. Then,

m(F_{s}^{*}\otimes F_{t}^{*})=\sum_{s\backslash t\leq_{T}u\leq_{T}s/t}F_{u}^{*}.

In
[
AS06
]
the authors use the Tamari order to define a cofree basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
.

Definition 2.22
.

The monomial basis
\{M_{t}\}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
, is defined by
\displaystyle F_{s}=\sum_{t\geq_{T}s}M_{t}
. The monomial basis is well-defined and is indeed a basis because of the triangularity obtained via Möbius inversion.

To define the comultiplication on the monomial basis, we introduce the idea of global descent.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
t
be a binary tree of degree
n
. Its
global descent
set is
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)=\{i\in[n-1]:t=s/r\text{ for some }s,r\text{ and }\deg(s)=i\}
.

For example, when
t=\hbox to80.47pt{\vbox to46.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.23325pt\lower-6.09045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}

, we have that
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)=\{1,4\}

Proposition 2.24

(Theorems 5.1, 4.2, and 6.1
[
AS06
]
)
.

(1)

The coproduct of monomial
basis elements is given by

\Delta_{+}(M_{t})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}M_{{}^{i}t}\otimes M_{t^{i}}.

(2)

The product of monomial basis elements has non-negative integer coefficients.

(3)

In the antipode image
\mathcal{S}(M_{t})
, all
terms have the same sign, equal to the parity of
|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)|+1
.

The references above give exact formulas for the multiplication and antipode; these may be obtained using Proposition
3.19
and the theorems in Section
3
.

2.4.
Permutations and weak order

In this article we view permutations via their correspondence to labeled binary trees (see for example
[
LR98
, Prop. 2.3]
).

Definition 2.25
.

A
permutation
of degree
n
is a labeled binary tree with
n
internal nodes, where the labeling is a bijection to the set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and such that the label of each internal node is smaller than any of the labels of its children. The set of permutations is denoted by
Perm
. The
descent
set of a permutation
\sigma
is the descent set of its underlying binary tree, denoted by
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)
. In Figure
1
we illustrate the set of permutations of
[3]
.

This definition is equivalent to the classical definition of a permutations as a bijection
\sigma:[n]\rightarrow[n]
. For a permutation
\sigma
, we define
\sigma(i)
to be the
i
-th label reading from left to right of the open area above the internal nodes. This is the reason we put the label in the area above the node. The descent set of
\sigma
can be equivalently defined in the classical definition as
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)=\{i:\sigma(i)>\sigma(i+1)\}
.

Definition 2.26
.

For a permutation
\sigma
, its
inversion
set is
\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma)=\{(i,j):i<j,\sigma(i)>\sigma(j)\}
. For permutations of
n
, we can construct a partial order by inclusion of inversion sets. This order is called the
(left) weak order
, denoted by
\leq_{w}
. (see for example Figure
7
)

The
inversed-inversion
set of
\sigma
is
\operatorname{\mathtt{iInv}}(\sigma)=\{(a,b):a<b,\sigma^{-1}(a)>\sigma^{-1}(b)\}
. The weak order can be equivalently defined as:
\sigma
is covered by
\tau
if
\tau=T_{a}\circ\sigma
and
|\operatorname{\mathtt{iInv}}(\tau)|=|\operatorname{\mathtt{iInv}}(\sigma)|+1
where
T_{a}=(a,a+1)
is the simple transposition.

2.5.
Hopf algebra of permutations

In this section, we recall the Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra of permutations
[
MR95
]
.

Definition 2.27
.

As a graded vector space, Malvenuto-Reutenauer Hopf algebra,
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
is
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Bbbk\text{Perm}_{n}
where
\text{Perm}_{n}
is the set of permutations of degree
n
. By convention
\text{Perm}_{0}
is the set containing the empty permutation. The fundamental basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
is
\{F_{\sigma}:\sigma\in\text{Perm}\}
.

The multiplication and comultiplication rules for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
are

F_{\sigma}\cdot F_{\tau}=\sum_{\tau\mapsto(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\deg(\sigma)+1})}F_{\sigma\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{\deg(\sigma)+1})},

\Delta(F_{\sigma})=\sum_{i=0}^{\deg(\sigma)}F_{{}^{i}\sigma}\otimes F_{\sigma^{i}}=\sum_{\sigma\mapsto(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})}F_{\operatorname{\mathtt{std}}(\sigma_{1})}\otimes F_{\operatorname{\mathtt{std}}(\sigma_{2})}.

It is known that the dual multiplication can be described using intervals on the weak order. (Note that
[
LR02
]
visualizes permutations as decreasing trees, so their definition of
\sigma\backslash\tau
is what we call
\sigma/\tau
, and vice versa.)

Proposition 2.29
.

[
LR02
]

Let
F_{\sigma}
and
F_{\tau}
be two permutations, and let
F_{\sigma}^{*}
,
F_{\tau}^{*}
be their dual basis element in the graded dual
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}^{*}
. Then,

m(F_{\sigma}^{*}\otimes F_{\tau}^{*})=\sum_{\sigma\backslash\tau\leq_{w}\rho\leq_{w}\sigma/\tau}F_{\rho}^{*}.

The weak order also leads to a cofree basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
.

Definition 2.30
.

[
AS05
]

The monomial basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
,
\{M_{\sigma}\}
, is defined using Möbius inversion by the formula
\displaystyle F_{\tau}=\sum_{\sigma\geq_{w}\tau}M_{\sigma}
.

In
[
AS05
]
, the authors provide formulas for the coproduct, product and antipode of these monomial basis elements. As in the case for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
,
the idea of global descent is important.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
\sigma
be a permutation of degree
n
. Its global descent set is
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)=\{i\in[n-1]:\sigma=\tau/\rho\text{ for some }\tau,\rho\text{ and }\deg(\tau)=i\}
. Equivalently,
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)=\{i\in[n-1]:\sigma(a)>\sigma(b)\text{ for all }a\leq i<b\}
.

For example, when
\sigma=\hbox to80.47pt{\vbox to50.77pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 40.23325pt\lower-6.09045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

, we have that
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)=\{4\}
, as indicated by the red branch.

Proposition 2.32
.

[Theorems 3.1, 4.1, and 5.5
[
AS05
]
]

(1)

The coproduct of monomial
basis elements in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
is given by

\Delta_{+}(M_{\sigma})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)}M_{{}^{i}\sigma}\otimes M_{\sigma^{i}}.

(2)

The product of monomial basis elements has non-negative
integer coefficients.

(3)

In the antipode image
\mathcal{S}(M_{\sigma})
, all
terms have the same sign, being the parity of
|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)|+1
.

In Examples
3.8
and
3.13
we illustrate how to calculate the product and the antipode in the monomial basis.

Recall from the end of Section
2.1
the forgetful projection
\pi:\text{LPT}\to\text{PT}
, which restricts to
\pi:\text{Perm}\to\text{PBT}
sending a permutation to its underlying binary tree. It follows from Proposition
2.12
that the projection
\Pi:\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
,
F_{\sigma}\mapsto F_{\pi(\sigma)}
is a Hopf morphism.

Remark 2.33
.

In
[
AS06
]
they construct
\Pi
by visualising permutations as decreasing trees instead. The two maps are equivalent by transforming our increasing trees into decreasing trees via the map
i\leftrightarrow n-i
on the labels. This is a Hopf morphism from
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
to a version of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
with multiplication in the opposite order, which explains why our version of
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
has multiplication in the opposite order from the one in
[
AS06
]
.

In the opposite direction, there is a section map
\iota:\text{PBT}\rightarrow\text{Perm}
, sending
t
to the unique
213
-avoiding permutation whose underlying tree is
t
. The construction is given at the end of Section
5.1
, and Lemma
5.25
shows that this is the maximal preimage of
t
under
\pi
.

We note additional well-known order-theoretic properties of the maps
\pi
and
\iota
, which are special cases of Proposition
5.26
and Lemma
5.32
regarding planar trees which we will prove in Section
5
.

Proposition 2.34
.

The maps
\pi:\text{Perm}\rightarrow\text{PBT}
and
\iota:\text{PBT}\rightarrow\text{Perm}
satisfy the following properties:

(1)

\pi
is order-preserving:
\sigma\leq_{w}\tau\implies\pi(\sigma)\leq_{T}\pi(\tau)
;

(2)

\iota
is order-preserving:
s\leq_{T}t\implies\iota(s)\leq_{w}\iota(t)
;

(3)

\pi
preserves the least upper bound:
\pi(\sigma\vee\tau)=\pi(\sigma)\vee\pi(\tau)
;

(4)

\iota
preserves global descents:
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)=\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\iota(t))
.

It follows from properties (1) and (2) above that
\pi
and
\iota
form a Galois connection (see
[
AS06
]
for the definition), which gives the following formula (
[
AS06
, Th. 3.1]
) for the images of monomial basis elements under the projection
\Pi
, which will also follow from our Theorem
3.16
below.

(3)

\Pi(M_{\sigma})=\begin{cases}M_{\pi(\sigma)}&\text{if }\sigma\text{ is $213$-avoiding},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

3.
Axioms for monomial basis properties

This section gives abstract conditions on the interaction between the poset of basis elements and the Hopf operations, for the behaviour of “monomial” bases seen in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
(Proposition
2.24
) and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
(Propositions
2.32
,
3.7
,
3.12
). We delay the proofs of the main theorems to Section
7
as they are technical. The ideas in this Section mimic the ones used in
[
AS05
,
AS06
]
.

Throughout this section, let
P_{n}
be a poset for each integer

n\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graded vector space with a pair of bases
\{F_{f}:f\in P_{n}\}
and
\{M_{f}:f\in P_{n}\}
,
related by
F_{f}=\sum_{g\geq f}M_{g}
. We will say that respectively they are the
fundamental
and
monomial
bases.

3.1.
Axioms for the coproduct formula

Here are the axioms necessary for the comultiplication of the monomial basis
to be “deconcatenation at global descents”.

(
\Delta
1).

\mathcal{H}
is a coalgebra and, for each
f\in P_{n}
, there exists a subset
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)\subseteq\{1,\dots,n-1\}

such that

(4)

\Delta_{+}(F_{f})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}F_{{}^{i}f}\otimes F_{f^{i}}

for some
{}^{i}f\in P_{i}
and
f^{i}\in P_{n-i}
; and if
f^{\prime}\geq f
,
then
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
. In particular,
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}
is constant over the connected components of
P_{n}
.

(
\Delta
2).

If
f\leq f^{\prime}\in P_{n}
, then
{}^{i}f\leq{}^{i}f^{\prime}
and
f^{i}\leq f^{\prime i}
.

(
\Delta
3).

Given
g\in P_{i}
,
h\in P_{n-i}

there exists a unique
g/h=\max\{f\in P_{n}|{}^{i}f=g,f^{i}=h\}
,
and, if
g\leq g^{\prime}\in P_{i}
,
h\leq h^{\prime}\in P_{n-i}
, then
g/h\leq g^{\prime}/h^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In axiom (
\Delta
1), in place of demanding that
\mathcal{H}
is already a coalgebra, we may instead specify conditions on the poset and the operations
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}
,
{}^{i}f
,
f^{i}
so that Equation (
4
) defines a coalgebra structure. These conditions are:

(1)

for
f\in P_{n}
and each pair
i<j\in[1,n-1]
, we have
j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
and
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)
if and only if
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
and
j-i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f^{i})
;

(2)

under the condition in (1),
{}^{i}\left({}^{j}f\right)={}^{i}f
,
\left({}^{j}f\right)^{i}={}^{j-i}\left(f^{i}\right)
;
f^{j}=\left(f^{i}\right)^{j-i}
.

Under the axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), we may define the global descent
set of each
f\in P_{n}
by

\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f):=\{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f):f={}^{i}f/f^{i}\}.

To describe iterated coproducts, it is necessary to define the deconcatenation
of
f
into more than two parts, an abstract analogue of the operation

t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
for trees. Given
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
,
let
f|S
be a
k
-tuple defined inductively
by
f|\{i\}:=({}^{i}f,f^{i})
and

(5)

f|\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j\}:=({}^{j}f|\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\},f^{j})

(for
i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}<j
; Lemma
7.1

will show that
S\backslash\{j\}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)
).). By coassociativity,
f|S
may be defined
in many equivalent ways.

Theorem 3.2
.

If
\mathcal{H}

is a coalgebra satisfying axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), then

(6)

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}(M_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}M_{{}^{i}f}\otimes M_{f^{i}},

(7)

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{[k]}(M_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f),\ |S|=k-1}M_{(f|S)_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes M_{(f|S)_{k}}.

3.2.
Axioms for the product formula

Next, we give axioms for the multiplication in a monomial basis to be computable
with the argument of
[
AS05
, Th. 4.1]
. One of the key ingredients
(condition (
m
1) below) is that the product of fundamental basis elements
should be computable by “shuffles”, which we abstract as follows:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
C_{i},C_{j}
be connected components of
P_{n},P_{m}

respectively. A
shuffle

\zeta
on
C_{i},C_{j}
is a
function
\zeta:C_{i}\times C_{j}\rightarrow P_{n+m}
.

Here is the complete list of relevant axioms:

(
m
0).

Each connected component of each
P_{n}
admits least upper bounds.

For any ordered pair of connected components
C_{i},C_{j}
of
P_{n},P_{m}

respectively, there is a set
Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
of shuffles such
that:

(
m
1).

\mathcal{H}
is an algebra and for
f\in C_{i}
and
g\in C_{j}
,

(8)

F_{f}F_{g}=\sum_{\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})}F_{\zeta(f,g)}.

(
m
2).

For
f\leq f^{\prime}\in C_{i}
and
g\leq g^{\prime}\in C_{j}
and
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
,
we have
\zeta(f,g)\leq\zeta(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
.

(
m
3).

For
f_{1},f_{2}\in C_{i}
and
g_{1},g_{2}\in C_{j}
and
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
,
we have
\zeta(f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2})\leq\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\zeta(f_{2},g_{2})
.

Fix
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
.
It follows from (
m
2) that for all
f\in C_{i}
and all
g\in C_{j}
,
the elements
\zeta(f,g)
all lie in the same connected component. Denote this output component
by
\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{i},C_{j}))
. Then we may define inductively

(9)

\displaystyle Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})

\displaystyle:=\{(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}):\zeta\in Sh(C_{j_{1}},C_{j_{2}}),\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{j_{1}},C_{j_{2}})),C_{j_{3}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})\},

\displaystyle(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})

\displaystyle:=\zeta^{\prime}(\zeta(f_{1},f_{2}),f_{3},\dots,f_{k}).

Remark 3.4
.

Similar to Remark
3.1
, we may replace the requirement in axiom (
m
1) that
\mathcal{H}
is an algebra by the following condition on shuffles
for
f\in C_{i},g\in C_{j},h\in C_{k}
:

\displaystyle\{\zeta^{\prime}(\zeta(f,g),h):\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j}),\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{i},C_{j})),C_{k})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\zeta^{\prime}(f,\zeta(g,h)):\zeta\in Sh(C_{j},C_{k}),\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(C_{i},\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{j},C_{k})))\}.

This ensures Equation (
8
) defines an associative multiplication.

Example 3.5
.

For
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
, we consider the weak order (Section
2.4
) and the Tamari order (Section
2.2
), respectively.
In each case,
C_{n}=P_{n}
is a unique connected component.
The set
Sh(C_{n},C_{m})
can be identified with the set of sequences
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{n}\leq m+1
.
For
\zeta\in Sh(C_{n},C_{m})
we define
\zeta:C_{n}\times C_{m}\rightarrow P_{n+m}

as
\zeta(f,g)=f\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
,
where
g\mapsto(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})
is the lightening splitting of
g
at the leaf positions
given by
\zeta
.

Under the axioms (
m
0)-(
m
3), the product coefficients for a monomial basis
is calculated as follows: given
f\in C_{i}
and
g\in C_{j}
, set

(10)

A_{f,g}^{h}:=\left\{\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})\middle|\>\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta(f,g)\leq h,\\
\mbox{and if }f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g\mbox{ satisfy }\zeta(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\leq h,\\
\mbox{then }f^{\prime}=f,g^{\prime}=g.\end{array}\right\},

and, given
f_{1}\in C_{1},f_{2}\in C_{2},\dots,f_{k}\in C_{k}
,
set

(11)

A_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}:=\left\{\zeta\in Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})\middle|\>\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\leq h,\\
\mbox{and if }f_{i}^{\prime}\geq f_{i}\mbox{ satisfy }\zeta(f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{k})\leq h,\\
\mbox{then }f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}\text{ for all }i.\end{array}\right\}.

Example
3.8
below shows how to calculate
A_{f,g}^{h}
for permutations.
To see what the coefficients
\alpha_{f,g}^{h}=|A_{f,g}^{h}|
look like for the tree-based
algebras in this paper, see Proposition
3.7
below.

Theorem 3.6
.

If
\mathcal{H}
is
an algebra satisfying axioms (
m
0)-(
m
3), then, for any
f\in P_{i}
and
g\in P_{n-i}
,

M_{f}\cdot M_{g}=\sum_{h\in P_{n}}\alpha_{f,g}^{h}M_{h}

where
\alpha_{f,g}^{h}=|A_{f,g}^{h}|
given in (
10
). Moreover, for any
integer
k>0
and
f_{i}\in P_{n_{i}}
with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
, we have

M_{f_{1}}\cdot M_{f_{2}}\cdots M_{f_{k}}=\sum_{h\in P_{n}}\alpha_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}M_{h}

where
\alpha_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}=|A_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}|
given in (
11
).

For our algebras of trees, the theorem above takes a form similar to the following proposition.

Proposition 3.7
.

[
AS05
, Th. 4.1]

The product of monomial basis elements in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
has non-negative
integer coefficients. More specifically, if
\sigma
has
k
leaves,
then
M_{\sigma}\cdot M_{\tau}=\sum_{\rho}\alpha_{\sigma,\tau}^{\rho}M_{\rho}
,
where
\alpha_{\sigma,\tau}^{\rho}
is the number of splittings
\tau\mapsto(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k})
that satisfy the following
conditions:

(1)

\sigma\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{k})\leq_{w}\rho
;

(2)

for
\sigma^{\prime}\geq_{w}\sigma
and
\tau^{\prime}\geq_{w}\tau
, if

\tau^{\prime}\mapsto(\tau^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{k})
satisfies
\deg\tau^{\prime}_{i}=\deg\tau_{i}

and
\sigma^{\prime}\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\tau^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\tau^{\prime}_{k})\leq_{w}\rho
,
then
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma
and
\tau^{\prime}=\tau
.

Example 3.8
.

We compute
\alpha_{\sigma,\tau}^{\rho}
, the
coefficient of
M_{\rho}
in the product
M_{\sigma}\cdot M_{\tau}
,
for
\sigma=\hbox to23.56pt{\vbox to22.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.78091pt\lower-6.09045pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 -7.87 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 15.75 15.75}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L -15.75 15.75}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\alpha_{\sigma,\tau}^{\rho}=1
.

3.3.
Axioms for the antipode formula

In the following, we require that
\mathcal{H}
is a Hopf algebra satisfying axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3) and (
m
0)-(
m
3).

Remark 3.9
.

Similar to Remarks
3.1
and
3.4
, we may translate the requirement that
\mathcal{H}
is a Hopf algebra into the following conditions on the poset operations: for
x\in C,y\in C^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\{^{i}(\zeta(x,y))|\zeta\in Sh(C,C^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle=\{\zeta^{\prime}({}^{j}x,{}^{i-j}y)|\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}({}^{j}x),\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}({}^{i-j}y)),j\in[1,i-1]\};

\displaystyle\{(\zeta(x,y))^{i}|\zeta\in Sh(C,C^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle=\{\zeta^{\prime}(x^{j},y^{i-j})|\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(x^{j}),\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(y^{i-j})),j\in[1,i-1]\}.

This will ensure the compatibility between the multiplication and comultiplication, defined by Equations
8
and
4
.

Fix
f\in P_{n}
and
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. Let
f|S=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
denote the splitting of
f
at
S

and let
C_{i}
denote the connected component containing
f_{i}
, then we write
\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S)

for the sequence
(C_{1},\dots,C_{k})
. From (
9
), each fixed shuffle
\zeta\in Sh(C_{1},C_{2})

defines an injection

\displaystyle Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{1},C_{2})),C_{3},\dots,C_{k})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow Sh(C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},\dots,C_{k})

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\,.

Similarly, by associativity of the multiplication, each fixed shuffle
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{i+1})

defines an injection

Sh(C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta(C_{i},C_{i+1})),C_{i+2},\dots,C_{k})\hookrightarrow Sh(C_{1},\dots,C_{k}).

Takeuchi’s antipode formula involves shuffles of
f|S
for different
S
; we need to compare shuffles of
f|S
with shuffles of
f|R
in order to resolve the cancelation of terms. To do so, Axiom (
\mathcal{S}0
) below will ask for a well chosen
\zeta_{*}\in Sh(C,C^{\prime})
such that the operation defined by

f\backslash g=\zeta_{*}(f,g)
is associative and
f\backslash g\leq f/g
for
f/g
defined by (
\Delta
3).
Lemma
7.4

will then show that the composition of injections as above gives well-defined
maps

\iota_{R,S}:Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))\hookrightarrow Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S)),

whenever
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. As an example: if
S=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\}

and
R=\{i_{1},i_{3},i_{4}\}
, then
\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))
takes
as input four entries. Then
\iota_{R,S}(\zeta)\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))

is defined by

(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(h_{1},\dots,h_{7})=\zeta(h_{1},h_{2}\backslash h_{3},h_{4},h_{5}\backslash h_{6}\backslash h_{7}).

(
\mathcal{S}
0).

There is a binary associative operation
(f,g)\mapsto f\backslash g

such that, for any connected components
C_{i}
and
C_{j}
we have a choice of
\zeta_{*}\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
that gives
f\backslash g=\zeta_{*}(f,g)
for all
f\in C_{i}
and
g\in C_{j}
.
Furthermore,
f\backslash g\leq f/g
.
Then we require that for any
R_{1},R_{2}\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, if
\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))

satisfies
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1},S}(\zeta_{1})=\iota_{R_{2},S}(\zeta_{2})

for some
\zeta_{1}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R_{1}))
and
\zeta_{2}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R_{2}))
,
then it is possible to find
\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R_{1}\cap R_{2}))
such that
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(\zeta^{\prime})
.

(
\mathcal{S}
1).

If
f^{\prime}\geq f\in P_{n}
, then
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f^{\prime})\supseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
;

(
\mathcal{S}
2).

If
f_{i}^{\prime}\geq f_{i}\in P_{n_{i}}
for each
i
, then
f^{\prime}_{1}/\cdots/f^{\prime}_{k}\leq f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}\vee f^{\prime}_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{k}
.

(
\mathcal{S}
3).

For any
i_{1}<j_{1}<i_{2}<j_{2}<\cdots<i_{l}<j_{l}
,

\displaystyle f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\left[(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{i_{1}})\backslash f_{i_{1}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f_{j_{1}-1}\backslash(f_{j_{1}}/\cdots/f_{i_{2}})\backslash f_{i_{2}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f_{j_{2}-1}\backslash\cdots\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\vee\left[f_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f_{i_{1}-1}\backslash(f_{i_{1}}/\cdots/f_{j_{1}})\backslash f_{j_{1}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f_{i_{2}-1}\backslash(f_{i_{2}}/\cdots/f_{j_{2}})\backslash\cdots\right].

Under these conditions, the antipode formula for monomial basis elements
involves the following quantity: given
f,h\in P_{n}
, let
\beta_{f}^{h}

be the number of
\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)))
satisfying the following three
conditions:

(12)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{(i)}&\zeta(f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f))\leq h;\\
\text{(ii)}&\mbox{if }f^{\prime}\geq f\mbox{ satisfies }\zeta(f^{\prime}|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f))\leq h,\mbox{ then }f=f^{\prime};\\
\text{(iii)}&\mbox{if }\zeta=\iota_{\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\},\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}\zeta^{\prime}\\
&\qquad\text{ for some }i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\text{ and }\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\})),\\
&\mbox{then }\zeta^{\prime}(f|(\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\}))\not\leq h.\end{array}

Theorem 3.10
.

If
\mathcal{H}
is a connected
Hopf algebra satisfying conditions (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), (
m
0)-(
m
3) and (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3), then the antipode is given by

\mathcal{S}(M_{f})=(-1)^{|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)|+1}\sum_{h}\beta_{f}^{h}M_{h}

where
\beta_{f}^{h}
is defined in (
12
).

Remark 3.11
.

In this paper we are particularly interested in Hopf algebras with bases indexed by special types of labeled trees.
When the labeling is closed under the operations defined in Definition
2.9
, we have that such algebras will satisfy (
\mathcal{S}
0).
To see this, we have
\zeta=\iota_{R,S}\zeta^{\prime}
if and only if, in
\zeta(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})
,
all nodes of
h_{i}
occur to the left of
h_{i+1}
, for each

i
where
\deg h_{1}+\cdots+\deg h_{i}\in S\backslash R
. (This
observation is used towards the end of Example
3.13
.) So if
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1},S}(\zeta_{1})=\iota_{R_{2},S}(\zeta_{2})
,
then, in
\zeta(h_{1},\dots,h_{k})
, all nodes of
h_{i}
occur
to the left of
h_{i+1}
, for each
i
where
\deg h_{1}+\cdots+\deg h_{i}\in(S\backslash R_{1})\cup(S\backslash R_{2})=S\backslash(R_{1}\cap R_{2})
.

Proposition 3.12
.

[
AS05
, Th. 5.5]

In the antipode of monomial basis elements in

\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
, all terms have the same sign. More specifically, if
\sigma\mapsto(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})

is the result of splitting
\sigma
at all its global descents, then

\mathcal{S}(M_{\sigma})=(-1)^{\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)+1}\sum_{\tau}\gamma_{\sigma}^{\tau}M_{\tau}

where
\gamma_{\sigma}^{\tau}
is the number of splitting sets
\sigma_{2}\mapsto(\sigma_{2}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{2}^{(k_{2})}),\sigma_{3}\mapsto(\sigma_{3}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{3}^{(k_{3})}),\dots,\sigma_{k}\mapsto(\sigma_{k}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{k}^{(k_{k})})

satisfying the following conditions:

(1)

\Big(\cdots\big((\sigma_{1}\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{2}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{2}^{(k_{2})}))\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{3}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{3}^{(k_{3})})\big)\cdots\Big)\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{k}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{k}^{(k_{k})})\leq_{w}\tau;
and for now on we always assume that such iterated shuffle is performed from left to right and will not put the parenthesis.

(2)

For each
\sigma^{\prime}>\sigma
, consider
{\sigma^{\prime}}\mapsto({\sigma^{\prime}}_{1},\dots,{\sigma^{\prime}}_{k})

and
{\sigma^{\prime}}_{i}\mapsto({\sigma^{\prime}}_{i}^{(1)},\dots,{\sigma^{\prime}}_{i}^{(k_{i})})
for
2\leq i\leq k

with
\deg{\sigma^{\prime}}_{i}^{(j)}=\deg{\sigma}_{i}^{(j)}
. Then

{\sigma^{\prime}}_{1}\overleftarrow{\shuffle}({\sigma^{\prime}}_{2}^{(1)},\dots,{\sigma^{\prime}}_{2}^{(k_{2})})\overleftarrow{\shuffle}({\sigma^{\prime}}_{3}^{(1)},\dots,{\sigma^{\prime}}_{3}^{(k_{3})})\cdots\overleftarrow{\shuffle}({\sigma^{\prime}}_{k}^{(1)},\dots,{\sigma^{\prime}}_{k}^{(k_{k})})\not\leq_{w}\tau;

(3)

Take any
i
where all nodes from
\sigma_{i}
appear to the left of all nodes from
\sigma_{i-1}
, so that the left hand side of (1)
may be computed as

\displaystyle\sigma_{1}\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{2}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{2}^{(k_{2})})\cdots

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{i-2}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{i-2}^{(k_{i-2})})

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\shuffle}((\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i})^{(1)},\dots,(\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i})^{(k^{\prime})})

\displaystyle\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{i+1}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{i+1}^{(k_{i+1})})\cdots\overleftarrow{\shuffle}(\sigma_{k}^{(1)},\dots,\sigma_{k}^{(k_{k})}),

for some splitting
\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i}\mapsto((\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i})^{(1)},\dots,(\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i})^{(k^{\prime})})
.
Replacing
\sigma_{i-1}\backslash\sigma_{i}
with
\sigma_{i-1}/\sigma_{i}
gives a result
\not\leq_{w}\tau
.

(1) to be computable.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.88092pt}{12.14201pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.19046pt}{17.83247pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\not\leq_{w}\tau
, so this lightening-split-choice satisfies (3).

Because a permutation is completely determined by its node labels,
without reference to the binary tree, the above calculation can be
entirely computed using the node labels only. (But the tree calculation
is necessary on similar algebras with unlabeled trees, or where the
node labels do not determine the tree.) Let
d_{i}=\deg\sigma_{1}+\cdots+\deg\sigma_{i}
,
i.e.
d_{i}
is the
i
th element of
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)
. If
\{d_{i-1}+1,d_{i-1}+2,\dots,d_{i}\}

appear setwise to the left of
\{d_{i}+1,d_{i}+2,\dots,d_{i+1}\}

(in the example, 2 and 3 are both to the left of 4), then “calculating
with
\sigma_{i}/\sigma_{i+1}
instead of
\sigma_{i}\backslash\sigma_{i+1}
”
amounts to replacing
d_{i}+1,d_{i}+2,\dots,d_{i+1}
with
d_{i-1}+1,d_{i-1}+2,\dots,d_{i-1}+(d_{i+1}-d_{i})

(e.g. 4 with 2) and
d_{i-1}+1,d_{i-1}+2,\dots,d_{i}
with
d_{i-1}+(d_{i+1}-d_{i})+1,\dots d_{i+1}

(e.g. 2, 3 with 3, 4). We check condition (3) for the shuffle on the
right of (
13
) using this expedited method. Here,
1 appears to the left of 2 and 3, and
3124\not\leq_{w}\tau
;
2 and 3 appear to the left of 4, and
1342\not\leq_{w}\tau
,
so this lightening-split-choice also satisfies (3).

Hence
\gamma_{\sigma}^{\tau}=2
.

3.4.
The inheritance of the axioms through subalgebras and quotienting

This section contains two theorems for transferring the above properties of monomial bases to subalgebras and quotient algebras.

In this section, we will
assume that
\mathcal{H}
is a connected
Hopf algebra satisfying conditions (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), (
m
0)-(
m
3) and (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3).

First, we consider subalgebras that are spanned by induced subposets:

Theorem 3.14
.

Assume that
Q_{n}\subseteq P_{n}
for each
n
, with the induced order, and
write
Q=\bigsqcup Q_{n}
.

•

If for all
f,g\in Q
, we have
{}^{i}f,f^{i}\in Q
for all

i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
and
f/g\in Q
, then
\operatorname{\mathtt{Span}}\{F_{f}:f\in Q\}
is
a subcoalgebra of
\mathcal{H}
satisfying (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3).

•

If for all
f,g\in Q
,
we have
\zeta(f,g)\in Q
for all
\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(f),\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(g))

and, when defined,
f\vee g\in Q
, then
\operatorname{\mathtt{Span}}\{F_{f}:f\in Q\}
is a subalgebra of

\mathcal{H}
satisfying (
m
0)-(
m
3).

•

If all above hypotheses on
Q
hold,
and for all
f,g\in Q
, we have
f\backslash g\in Q
. Then
\operatorname{\mathtt{Span}}\{F_{f}:f\in Q\}

satisfies (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3).

The proof is trivial. Note that, because
Q
is closed under “deconcatenation”
and “maximal concatenation”
/
, the meaning of
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}
is the
same in
Q
as in
P
. However, unless
Q
is obtained as a collection of upper order-ideals in
P
,
the monomial basis elements of
\operatorname{\mathtt{Span}}\{F_{f}:f\in Q\}
created using

Q
are not monomial basis elements of
\mathcal{H}
.

The next situation of inheritance of axioms is more technical but more interesting.
For example, it will be used to pass from labeled to unlabeled trees. For the rest
of this section, we will assume we have a second graded connected Hopf algebra
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
defined by a poset as follow.
Let
\bar{P}_{n}
be posets for each integer
n\geq 0
.
Assume that
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
, as a graded vector
space, has a fundamental basis
\{F_{t}:t\in\bar{P}_{n}\}
. In both
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
we define
a monomial basis
\{M_{f}:f\in P_{n}\}
(resp.
\{M_{t}:t\in\bar{P}_{n}\}
), related by
F_{f}=\sum_{g\geq f}M_{g}
(resp.
F_{t}=\sum_{s\geq t}M_{s}
).
Let
\pi_{n}:P_{n}\rightarrow\bar{P}_{n}
be a sequence of surjective order-preserving
maps, and let
\Pi:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{H}}
given
by
\Pi(F_{f})=F_{\pi_{\deg f}(f)}
be the induced linear map. One
key axiom that we need is the following:

(
\pi
0).

For each
n
, there exists maps
\iota_{n}:\bar{P}_{n}\rightarrow P_{n}

given by
\iota_{n}(t)=\max\{f\in P_{n}:\pi_{n}(f)=t\}
, and
\iota_{n}

is order-preserving.

Axiom (
\pi
0) requires that, for each
t\in\bar{P}_{n}
, it is possible to compute
\max\{f\in P_{n}:\pi_{n}(f)=t\}
. In particular,
\{f\in P_{n}:\pi_{n}(f)=t\}
must lie in the same connected component of
P_{n}
.
Axiom (
\pi
0) implies that
\pi_{n}
and
\iota_{n}
form a
Galois connection
, i.e.
\pi_{n}(f)\leq s
if and only if
f\leq\iota_{n}(s)
. It follows that the connected components
C_{i}
of
P_{n}
are in bijection with the connected components
\bar{C}_{i}
of
\bar{P}_{n}
. Since
\pi_{n}
is part of a Galois connection it preserves least upper bounds, which we record as the following lemma.

Lemma 3.15
.

If axiom (
\pi
0)
is satisfied, then
\pi_{n}(f\vee g)=\pi_{n}f\vee\pi_{n}g
for all

f,g\in C_{i}\subseteq P_{n}
. ∎

An immediate consequence of this Lemma is that (
m
0) is satisfied for
\bar{P}_{n}
.
The other relevant axioms that we require from
\pi
to be able to transfer axioms
(
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), (
m
0)-(
m
3), (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3) from
\mathcal{H}
to
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
are:

(
\pi\Delta
1).

for
t\in\bar{P}_{n}
, we have a map
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(t)\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
such that

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}(F_{t})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(t)}F_{{}^{i}t}\otimes F_{t^{i}}

for some
{}^{i}t\in\bar{P}_{i}
,
t^{i}\in\bar{P}_{n-i}
; and for all
f\in P_{n}
, we have
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)=\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(\pi_{n}f)
.

(
\pi\Delta
2).

\pi
commutes with deconcatenation:
{}^{i}(\pi_{n}f)=\pi_{i}\left({}^{i}f\right)
,

(\pi_{n}f)^{i}=\pi_{n-i}\left(f^{i}\right)
for
f\in P_{n}
.

(
\pi m
1).

For all connected components
\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j}
of
\bar{P}_{n},\bar{P}_{m}
, we have a set
Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})
of shuffles such that,
for
s\in\bar{C}_{i}
and
t\in\bar{C}_{j}
, we have
F_{s}F_{t}=\sum_{\bar{\zeta}\in Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})}F_{\bar{\zeta}(s,t)}
.
Axiom (
\pi
0) gives us corresponding connected components
{C}_{i},{C}_{j}
of
{P}_{n},{P}_{m}
and we require a
bijection
Sh(C_{i},C_{j})\rightarrow Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})
.

(
\pi m
2).

For all
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
, in bijection with
\bar{\zeta}\in Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})
, and for all
f\in C_{i}
,
g\in C_{j}
,
we have
\pi_{n}(\zeta(f,g))=\bar{\zeta}(\pi_{n}(f),\pi_{m}(g))
.

(
\pi\mathcal{S}
1).

\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\iota_{n}(t))=\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(t)
for
t\in\bar{P}_{n}
.

(
\pi\mathcal{S}
2).

\pi_{n}(g/h)=\pi_{i}(g)/\pi_{n-i}(h)
for all
g\in P_{i}
,
h\in P_{n-i}
.

Theorem 3.16
.

For
\mathcal{H},\bar{\mathcal{H}}
, if the maps
\pi_{n}\colon P_{n}\to\bar{P}_{n}

satisfy (
\pi
0), then

(14)

\Pi(M_{f})=\begin{cases}M_{t}&\text{if }f=\iota(t)\text{ for some }t;\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Furthermore:

•

If (
\pi\Delta
1)-(
\pi\Delta
2) are satisfied, then
\Pi
is a coalgebra morphism,
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
also satisfies axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), with

s/t
defined as
\pi_{n}(\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t))
for
s\in\bar{P}_{i}

and
t\in\bar{P}_{n-i}
.

•

If (
\pi m
1)-(
\pi m
2) are satisfied, then
\Pi
is an algebra morphism,
and
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
also satisfies (
m
0)-(
m
3).

•

If (
\pi\Delta
1)-(
\pi\Delta
2),
(
\pi m
1)-(
\pi m
2) and (
\pi\mathcal{S}
1)-(
\pi\mathcal{S}
2) are satisfied, then
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
also
satisfies (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3).

See Section 7.4 for the proof.

Remark 3.17
.

Note that this theorem transfers the full axioms from
\mathcal{H}
to
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
, in contrast to
[
AS06
]
where they only transfer the monomial basis formulas (for coproduct, product and antipode). The formula transfer uses only that
\pi_{n}
and
\iota_{n}
form a Galois connection, but transferring the axioms seems to require the stronger (
\pi
0) axiom.

3.5.
Axioms on
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}

Before using all the developed axiomatic machinery on new Hopf algebras we illustrate first how the axioms are satisfied by
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
. We will use that the axioms are satisfied by these two Hopf algebras in our construction of a Hopf algebra on parking functions in Section
6
.

Proposition 3.18
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
satisfies axioms
(
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), (
m
0)-(
m
3), (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3).

Proof.

Axiom (
\Delta
1) is clear. (
\Delta
2) follows from
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.5.i]
.
(
\Delta
3) follows from
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.5.ii]
: the first case shows
that
/
is order-preserving, and the third case gives the maximality.
That
/
is a one-sided inverse to
\sigma\mapsto({}^{i}\sigma,\sigma^{i})

is clear from the definitions.

Axiom (
m
0) is clear. Axiom (
m
1) is from the definition of the algebra (see Definition
2.27
). Axioms (
m
2) and (
m
3) are
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.10]
.

Axiom (
\mathcal{S}
0) follows from Remark
3.11
and (
\mathcal{S}
1)
follows from
[
AS05
, Lem. 2.14]
.

The intuition behind (
\mathcal{S}
2) is that all inversions of
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}/\cdots/\sigma^{\prime}_{k}

are either “within” an
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
, in which case they are
in
\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
, or “between”
two
\sigma^{\prime}_{i}
s, in which case they are in
\sigma_{1}/\cdots/\sigma_{k}
.
It may be rigorously deduced from
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.16.iii]
as
follows: define
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}/\cdots/\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
and
\sigma=\sigma_{1}/\cdots/\sigma_{k}

(here
\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
play the role of
f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}
).
By axiom
\Delta 3
,
\sigma^{\prime}\geq\sigma
, so
\sigma^{\prime}\vee\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
.
Let
R=\emptyset
and
S=\{\deg\sigma_{1}+\cdots+\deg\sigma_{i}:i\in\{1,\dots,k\}\}
;
note that
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\sigma)
and
\sigma|S=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})
.
Then
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.16.iii]
says
\sigma^{\prime}_{S}\vee\sigma_{R}=(\sigma\vee\sigma^{\prime})_{R\cap S}
,
where their notation
\sigma_{S}
denotes
\sigma_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash\sigma_{k}
.
Thus
\sigma_{R}=\sigma=\sigma_{1}/\cdots/\sigma_{k}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{S}=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
,

(\sigma\vee\sigma^{\prime})_{R\cap S}=\sigma\vee\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}_{1}/\cdots/\sigma^{\prime}_{k}
.

Axiom (
\mathcal{S}
3) also follows from
[
AS05
, Prop. 2.16.iii]
,
by taking
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma_{1}/\cdots/\sigma_{k}
,
R=\{d_{1},\dots,d_{i_{1}-1}\}\cup\{d_{j_{1}},\dots,d_{i_{2}-1}\}\cup\cdots\cup\{d_{j_{l-1}},\dots,d_{i_{l}-1}\}
,

S=\{d_{i_{1}},\dots,d_{j_{1}-1}\}\cup\{d_{i_{2}},\dots,d_{j_{2}-1}\}\cup\cdots\cup\{d_{i_{l}},\dots,d_{k}\}
,
where
d_{i}=\deg f_{1}+\cdots+\deg f_{i}
.
∎

Proposition 3.19
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
satisfies axioms
(
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3), (
m
0)-(
m
3), (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3).

Proof.

Use Theorem
3.16
to bootstrap from
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
,
as Proposition
2.34
provides the necessary
maps
\pi
and
\iota
satisfying (
\pi
0). It is clear that
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}

satisfies axioms (
\pi\Delta
1) and (
\pi m
1). Axioms (
\pi\Delta
2),
(
\pi m
2), (
\pi\mathcal{S}
1) and (
\pi\mathcal{S}
2) follow from Propositions

2.12
and
2.34
.
∎

4.
Hopf algebra of planar trees

4.1.
Two isomorphic Hopf structures

In this section, we define two Hopf structures on the free vector space spanned by planar trees.

Definition 4.1
.

As a graded vector space, the Hopf algebra of planar trees,
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
is
\displaystyle\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\Bbbk\text{PT}_{n}
where
\text{PT}_{n}
is the set of planar trees with
n+1
leaves. By convention
\text{PT}_{0}
is the span of the empty tree. We denote two bases for
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
by
\{G_{t}:t\in\text{PT}\}
and
\{F_{t}:t\in\text{PT}\}
.

Recall that a splitting
t\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2})
is
allowable
if
\text{ideg}(t)=\text{ideg}(t_{1})+\text{ideg}(t_{2})
. We can define two comultiplication structures on
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
.

\Delta^{A}(\tilde{F}_{t})=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2})}\tilde{F}_{t_{1}}\otimes\tilde{F}_{t_{2}},

\Delta^{B}(F_{t})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{allowable}\\
t\mapsto(t_{1},t_{2})\end{subarray}}F_{t_{1}}\otimes F_{t_{2}}.

Then, we can define two multiplication structures on
\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}}
. Let
s,t\in\text{PT}
,

m^{A}(\tilde{F}_{s}\otimes\tilde{F}_{t})=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})}\tilde{F}_{s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})},

m^{B}(F_{s}\otimes F_{t})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{allowable}\\
t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})\end{subarray}}F_{s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{\deg(s)+1})}.

It is not hard to see that both
(m^{A},\Delta^{A})
and
(m^{B},\Delta^{B})
preserve the degree, and
(m^{B},\Delta^{B})
preserves the internal degree.

Proposition 4.4
.

(\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}},m^{A},\Delta^{A})
and
(\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}},m^{B},\Delta^{B})
are both graded Hopf algebras with respect to the degree.
(\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}},m^{B},\Delta^{B})
is a graded Hopf algebra with respect to the internal degree as well (so it is bigraded).

Proof.

It is clear that
\Delta^{A}
and
\Delta^{B}
are both coassociative. To see associativity, let
r,s,t
be trees of degree
a-1,b-1,c-1
respectively, then for
(\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}},m^{A},\Delta^{A})
,

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{r}\cdot(\tilde{F}_{s}\cdot\tilde{F}_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{b})}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u=s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{b})\\
u\mapsto(u_{1},\dots,u_{a})\end{subarray}}\tilde{F}_{r\shuffle(u_{1},\dots,u_{a})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s\mapsto(s_{1},\dots,s_{a})\\
t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{a+b-1})\end{subarray}}\tilde{F}_{\left(r\shuffle(s_{1},\dots,s_{a})\right)\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{a+b-1})}

\displaystyle=(\tilde{F}_{r}\cdot\tilde{F}_{s})\cdot\tilde{F}_{t}.

The second equality can be visualized using the following example.

yields

.

It is left to see the compatibility. Let
s,t
be two trees,
\deg(s)=k
. We use the notation
\Delta_{i}(s)=\Delta_{i,k-i}(s)={}^{(i)}s\otimes s^{(k-i)}
, then for
(\operatorname{\mathsf{TSym}},m^{A},\Delta^{A})
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}(\tilde{F}_{s}\cdot\tilde{F}_{t})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k+1})}\Delta_{i}\tilde{F}_{s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{k+1})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\mapsto(t_{1},\dots,t_{k+2})}\sum_{\deg(t_{1})+\cdots+\deg(t_{n+1})=i-n}\tilde{F}_{{}^{(n)}s\shuffle(t_{1},\dots,t_{n+1})}\otimes\tilde{F}_{s^{(k-n)}\shuffle(t_{n+2},\dots,t_{k+2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{i}\Delta_{n}(\tilde{F}_{s})\cdot\Delta_{i-n}(\tilde{F}_{t}).

The second equality can be visualized using the following example.
