Lemma 6.10
.

The poset
\leq_{P}
admits least upper bounds. More specifically, given two parking functions
f
and
g
of the same degree,
f\vee g
is the parking function
(\pi_{\text{perm}}f\vee\pi_{\text{perm}}g,\pi_{\text{tree}}f\vee\pi_{\text{tree}}g)
.

Proof.

We first make the following claims.

(1)

If
s,t
are two binary trees of the same degree, then
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s\vee t)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)
,

(2)

If
\sigma,\tau
are two permutations of the same degree, then
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma\vee\tau)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)
.

First note that
(2)\implies(1)
: let
\sigma=\iota(s)
and
\tau=\iota(t)
so that
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\pi\sigma)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s)
and similarly
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)
. So
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma\vee\tau)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\pi(\sigma\vee\tau))=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\pi\sigma\vee\pi\tau)=\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s\vee t)
, using Proposition
2.34
.(3) in the second to last equality.

To see
(2)
, first note that from the definition of weak order we have that
\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\tau)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma\vee\tau)
. In particular, it must also hold that
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma\vee\tau)
.

Conversely, as was noted in
[
M94
]
,
\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma\vee\tau)
is the transitive closure of
\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\tau)
i.e.
(i,j),(j,k)\in\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma\vee\tau)\implies(i,k)\in\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma\vee\tau)
. Since
(i,i+1)
cannot come from transitivity of other inversions, we must have
(i,i+1)\in\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma\vee\tau)\implies(i,i+1)\in\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma)\cup\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\tau)
.

Let
f=(\sigma,s)
and
g=(\tau,t)
. By definition, we know that
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)
and
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)
. Therefore, by the claims,
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(s\vee t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma\vee\tau)
i.e.
(\sigma\vee\tau,s\vee t)
is a parking function. Then it is obviously
f\vee g
.
∎

Let
f,g
be two parking functions. It is not hard to see that
f/g
and
f\backslash g
are parking functions, since the only new descent in the underlying tree is
\deg(f)
in
f/g
, and it is also a descent in the corresponding permutation because the labels in
f
are shifted up. In
f\backslash g
there are no new descents.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
f
and
g
be two parking functions, and let
F_{f}^{*}
,
F_{g}^{*}
be the dual basis element in the graded dual
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}^{*}
. Then,

m(F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*})=\sum_{f\backslash g\leq_{P}h\leq_{P}f/g}F_{h}^{*}.

Proof.

Fix a parking function
h
and
0\leq i\leq\deg(h)
. Let
\Delta_{i}(F_{h})=F_{{}^{i}h}\otimes F_{h^{i}}
. It follows immediately from the definition that we have
\langle F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*},\Delta_{i}(F_{h})\rangle=1
for a unique pair
(f,g)
, or
\langle F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*},\Delta_{i}(F_{h})\rangle=0
otherwise. We claim that the following are equivalent.

(1)

\langle F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*},\Delta_{i}(F_{h})\rangle=1
.

(2)

(a)

\langle F_{\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)}^{*}\otimes F_{\pi_{\text{perm}}(g)}^{*},\Delta_{i}(F_{\pi_{\text{perm}}(h)})\rangle=1
and

(b)

\langle F_{\pi_{\text{tree}}(f)}^{*}\otimes F_{\pi_{\text{tree}}(g)}^{*},\Delta_{i}(F_{\pi_{\text{tree}}(h)})\rangle=1
.

(3)

(a)

\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)\backslash\pi_{\text{perm}}(g)\leq_{w}\pi_{\text{perm}}(h)\leq_{w}\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)/\pi_{\text{perm}}(g)
and

(b)

\pi_{\text{tree}}(f)\backslash\pi_{\text{tree}}(g)\leq_{T}\pi_{\text{tree}}(h)\leq_{T}\pi_{\text{tree}}(f)/\pi_{\text{tree}}(g)
.

(c)

\deg(f)=i

(4)

f\backslash g\leq_{P}h\leq_{P}f/g
and
\deg(f)=i
.

The equivalence of (2) and (3) follows from Propositions
2.21
and
2.29
. The equivalence of (3) and (4) follows from the easy facts
\pi_{\text{perm}}(f/g)=\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)/\pi_{\text{perm}}(g)
and other similar relation for binary trees.
(1)\Rightarrow(2)
follows immediately from the definition of
\Delta_{i}(h)
. Given
(2)
, the 4-tuple of permutations and binary trees
(\pi_{\text{perm}}(f),\pi_{\text{perm}}(g),\pi_{\text{tree}}(f),\pi_{\text{tree}}(g))
is unique. The uniqueness leads to (1) because we know that there exists
f,g
satisfying (1) and therefore satisfying (2).

The equivalent of (1) and (4), together with the relation
\langle m(F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*}),F_{h}\rangle=\langle F_{f}^{*}\otimes F_{g}^{*},\Delta(F_{h})\rangle=0
or
1
, completes the proof.
∎

Definition 6.12
.

The monomial basis of parking functions are defined as the elements satisfying
\displaystyle F_{f}=\sum_{g\geq_{P}f}M_{g}
. The monomial basis is unique by triangularity and it forms a basis via Möbius inversion.

Definition 6.13
.

Let
f
be a parking function of degree
n
. Its global descent set
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
is
\{i\in[n-1]:i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\pi_{\text{perm}}(f))\cap\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\pi_{\text{tree}}(f))\}
.

Proposition 6.14
.

The coproduct
in the monomial basis of
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
is:

\Delta_{+}(M_{f})=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}M_{{}^{i}f}\otimes M_{f^{i}}.

Proof.

We check the axioms in Theorem
3.2
. Axiom
(
\Delta
1) is immediate because
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)=\{1,\dots,\deg f-1\}
).

To check (
\Delta
2): if
f\leq_{P}f^{\prime}
, then
\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)\leq_{w}\pi_{\text{perm}}(f^{\prime})
,
so by condition
\Delta 3
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
, we have
\pi_{\text{perm}}({}^{i}f)={}^{i}(\pi_{\text{perm}}f)\leq_{w}{}^{i}(\pi_{\text{perm}}f^{\prime})=\pi_{\text{perm}}({}^{i}f^{\prime})
,
and similarly
\pi_{\text{tree}}({}^{i}f)\leq_{T}\pi_{\text{tree}}({}^{i}f^{\prime})
,
so
{}^{i}f\leq_{P}{}^{i}f^{\prime}
. By the same argument, we also
have
f^{i}\leq_{P}f^{\prime i}
.

To check (
\Delta
3): it follows from Proposition
6.11
that
g/h=\max\{f|{}^{i}f=g,f^{i}=h\}
. That
/
is order-preserving is easily proved using a similar argument to (
\Delta
2) above.

Finally, we check that Definition
6.13

of a global descent agrees with the definition in Section
3.1
.
For this, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
.

(2)

i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\pi_{\text{perm}}f)
and
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(\pi_{\text{tree}}f)
.

(3)

\pi_{\text{perm}}f={}^{i}(\pi_{\text{perm}}f)/(\pi_{\text{perm}}f)^{i}=\pi_{\text{perm}}({}^{i}f/f^{i})
,
\pi_{\text{tree}}f={}^{i}(\pi_{\text{tree}}f)/(\pi_{\text{tree}}f)^{i}=\pi_{\text{tree}}({}^{i}f/f^{i})
.

(4)

f={}^{i}f/f^{i}
.

The equivalence of (1) and (2) is Definition
6.13
.
The equivalence of (2) and (3) is the definition of global descent
from Theorem
3.2
, in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
.
The equivalence of (3) and (4) is because
f
is uniquely determined
by its images under
\pi_{\text{perm}}
and
\pi_{\text{tree}}
.
∎

Proposition 6.15
.

The product
of monomial basis elements in
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
is given by
M_{f}\cdot M_{g}=\sum_{h}\alpha_{f,g}^{h}M_{h}
,
where
\alpha_{f,g}^{h}=|A_{f,g}^{h}|
is defined as in (
10
).

Proof.

We check the axioms in Theorem
3.6
. Axioms
(
m
0) follows from Lemma
6.10
. Axiom (
m
1) follows from
the definition of multiplication in
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
using shuffles as defined in Example
3.5
. Axiom (
m
2) may be verified in the
same manner as for (
\Delta
2) in the proof of Proposition
6.14
,
i.e. by applying axiom (
m
2) for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
to the images
of
f
and
g
under
\pi_{\text{perm}}
and
\pi_{\text{tree}}
.

To check (
m
3), first observe that
\pi_{\text{perm}}\zeta(f,g)=\zeta(\pi_{\text{perm}}f,\pi_{\text{perm}}g)

and similarly for
\pi_{\text{tree}}
. Using this, together with Lemma

6.10
in the second and fifth lines, and condition
m
4 for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
in the third line,

\displaystyle\pi_{\text{perm}}\zeta(f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta(\pi_{\text{perm}}(f_{1}\vee f_{2}),\pi_{\text{perm}}(g_{1}\vee g_{2}))

\displaystyle=\zeta(\pi_{\text{perm}}f_{1}\vee\pi_{\text{perm}}f_{2},\pi_{\text{perm}}g_{1}\vee\pi_{\text{perm}}g_{2})

\displaystyle\leq_{w}\zeta(\pi_{\text{perm}}f_{1},\pi_{\text{perm}}g_{1})\vee\zeta(\pi_{\text{perm}}f_{2},\pi_{\text{perm}}g_{2})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\text{perm}}\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\pi_{\text{perm}}\zeta(f_{2},g_{2})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\text{perm}}(\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\zeta(f_{2},g_{2})).

By the same argument,
\pi_{\text{tree}}\zeta(f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2})\leq_{T}\pi_{\text{tree}}(\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\zeta(f_{2},g_{2}))
,
so
\zeta(f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2})\leq_{P}\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\zeta(f_{2},g_{2})
.
∎

Proposition 6.16
.

The
antipode of monomial basis elements in
\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}
is given by
\mathcal{S}(M_{f})=(-1)^{\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)+1}\sum_{g}\beta_{f}^{g}M_{g}

where
\beta_{f}^{g}
is defined as in (
12
).

Proof.

We check the axioms for Theorem
3.10
. Axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3)
and (
m
0)-(
m
3) were already checked above.

Axiom (
\mathcal{S}
0) follows from Remark
3.11
. Axiom (
\mathcal{S}
1)
follows readily from Definition
6.13
of

\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, together with condition (
\mathcal{S}
1) for
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
. Axioms (
\mathcal{S}
2) and (
\mathcal{S}
3) can be checked like
for (
\Delta
2) in the proof of Proposition
6.14
:
apply the corresponding axioms in
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}
to the images
of
f_{i}
under
\pi_{\text{perm}}
and
\pi_{\text{tree}}
.
∎

Proposition 6.17
.

The Hopf projections
\Pi_{\text{tree}}:\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathcal{Y}Sym}}

and
\Pi_{\text{perm}}:\operatorname{\mathsf{PSym}}\rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{\mathfrak{S}Sym}}
on the monomial basis is given
by:

\Pi_{\text{tree}}(M_{f})=\begin{cases}M_{\pi_{\text{tree}}(f)}&\text{if }\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)=n\ n-1\ \cdots\ 1;\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

\Pi_{\text{perm}}(M_{f})=\begin{cases}M_{\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)}&\text{if }f=\max\{g:\pi_{\text{perm}}(g)=\pi_{\text{perm}}(f)\};\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Use Theorem
3.16
: by definition of
\leq_{P}
,
both
\pi_{\text{tree}}
and
\pi_{\text{perm}}
are order-preserving. Following
axiom (
\pi
0),

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{tree}}(t)

\displaystyle=\max\{(\sigma,t)\text{ a parking function}\}

\displaystyle=(\max\{\sigma:\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\},t)=(n\ n-1\ \cdots\ 1,t),

and
\iota_{\text{tree}}
is clearly order-preserving. Similarly,

\displaystyle\iota_{\text{perm}}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\max\{(\sigma,t)\text{ a parking function}\}

\displaystyle=(\sigma,\max\{t:\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\}),

which exists by claim (1) in the proof of Lemma
6.10
.
To show
\iota_{\text{perm}}
is order-preserving, we show
that, if
\sigma\geq_{w}\tau
, then
\max\{t:\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\}\geq_{T}\max\{t:\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)\}
.
This is true because
\sigma\geq_{w}\tau
means
\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\sigma)\supseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Inv}}(\tau)

so
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)\supseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\tau)
.
∎

Remark 6.18
.

Our bijection from classical parking functions to our labeled-tree visualization can be extended to
m
-parking functions; the resulting
m+1
-ary trees have labels satisfying
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)
for some generalized definition of descent. With a multiplication and comultiplication analogous to that of
\operatorname{\mathsf{STSym}}
, these
m
-parking functions form a Hopf algebra, that is likely to be isomorphic to the one defined in
[
NT20
]
. By imposing the condition
\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(t)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{Des}}(\sigma)
on labeled trees of any arity, we obtain a Hopf algebra of “planar parking functions”, which contains the
m
-parking function algebras.

7.
Proofs of the monomial bases properties

Throughout this section, let
P_{n}
be a poset for each integer

n\geq 0
. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a graded vector space with a fundamental
basis
\{F_{f}:f\in P_{n}\}
and a monomial basis
\{M_{f}:f\in P_{n}\}
,
related by
F_{f}=\sum_{g\geq f}M_{g}
. Let
C_{j}
denote a connected
component of a
P_{n}
.

7.1.
Proof of the coproduct formulas in the monomial basis

Proving the iterated coproduct formula (
7
)
and the antipode formula require the following
k
-factor generalizations
of the comultiplication axioms. Recall that for
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
, we denote by
f|S
the splitting of
f
at
S
.

(
\Delta 1^{\prime}
).

For each
f\in P_{n}
,

(16)

\Delta_{+}^{[k]}(F_{f})=\sum_{S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f),\ |S|=k-1}F_{(f|S)_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{(f|S)_{k}}.

(
\Delta 2^{\prime}
).

If
f\leq f^{\prime}\in P_{n}
, then
f|S\leq f^{\prime}|S
componentwise.

(
\Delta 3^{\prime}
).

Given
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}
with
f_{i}\in P_{n_{i}}
, let

S=\{n_{1},n_{1}+n_{2},\dots,n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k-1}\}
, and
n=n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
.
Then

f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}=\max\{f\in P_{n}:f|S=(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})\}.

If
f_{i}\leq f^{\prime}_{i}
for all
i
, then

f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}\leq f_{1}^{\prime}/\cdots/f_{k}^{\prime};

and
/
is an associative operation.

(
\Delta 4^{\prime}
).

Given
f\in P_{n}
and
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
with
|S|=k-1
and

f|S=(f_{1},\dots f_{k})
, we have
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
if and only
if
f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}

satisfies axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3). Then, for each
f\in P_{n}
and each

i<j
with
j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
, we have
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
if and only
if
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)
. And
\mathcal{H}

satisfies (
\Delta 1^{\prime}
)-(
\Delta 4^{\prime}
) for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

Calculate
\Delta_{+}^{[3]}(F_{f})
in two ways:
by coassociativity,

(17)

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{[3]}(F_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\Delta_{+}F_{{}^{j}f}\otimes F_{f^{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)}F_{{}^{i}\left({}^{j}f\right)}\otimes F_{\left({}^{j}f\right)^{i}}\otimes F_{f^{j}},

where the degrees of
F_{{}^{i}\left({}^{j}f\right)}
,
F_{\left({}^{j}f\right)^{i}}
,
F_{f^{j}}

are respectively
i
,
j-i
,
n-j
, and

(18)

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{[3]}(F_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}F_{{}^{i}f}\otimes\Delta_{+}F_{f^{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\sum_{j^{\prime}\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f^{i})}F_{{}^{i}f}\otimes F_{{}^{j^{\prime}}\left(f^{i}\right)}\otimes F_{\left(f^{i}\right)^{j^{\prime}}},

where the degrees of
F_{{}^{i}f}
,
F_{{}^{j^{\prime}}\left(f^{i}\right)}
,
F_{\left(f^{i}\right)^{j^{\prime}}}

are respectively
i
,
j^{\prime}
,
n-i-j^{\prime}
. We have
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
if and only if there is a non-term with first tensor of degree
i
in (
18
).
The equivalence of (
17
) and (
18
) shows that
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
if and only if
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)
.

To see (
\Delta 1^{\prime}
): because
\Delta_{+}^{[k]}=(\Delta_{+}^{[k-1]}\otimes\iota)\circ\Delta_{+}
:

\displaystyle\Delta_{+}^{[k]}(F_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\Delta_{+}^{[k-1]}F_{{}^{j}f}\otimes F_{f^{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k-2}\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)}F_{({}^{j}f|\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k-2}\})_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes F_{({}^{j}f|\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k-2}\})_{k-1}}\otimes F_{f^{j}},

by induction on
k
.
By the equivalence of (
17
) and (
18
), we have the equivalent conditions

j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)\text{ and }i_{1},\dots,i_{k-2}\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}({}^{j}f)\Longleftrightarrow i_{1},\dots,i_{k-2},j\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f).

Now use definition (
5
).

Axiom (
\Delta 2^{\prime}
) is easy to show by induction on
|S|
, using (
5
).

For (
\Delta 3^{\prime}
), to see the associativity of
/
,
first consider the case
k=3
, so

(19)

(f_{1}/f_{2})/f_{3}=\max\left\{f:{}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}f=(f_{1}/f_{2}),f^{n_{1}+n_{2}}=f_{3}\right\}.

Consider
g=\max\left\{f:f|S=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\right\}
. Since

((f_{1}/f_{2})/f_{3})|S=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
, so
g\geq(f_{1}/f_{2})/f_{3}
.
By (
\Delta
2), this implies

{}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}g\geq{}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}((f_{1}/f_{2})/f_{3})=f_{1}/f_{2}.

Separately, by (
17
),
{}^{n_{1}}({}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}g)=f_{1}

and
({}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}g)^{n_{1}}=f_{2}
, so by definition of
f_{1}/f_{2}
,
we have
{}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}g\leq f_{1}/f_{2}
, which implies
{}^{n_{1}+n_{2}}g=f_{1}/f_{2}
.
Hence,
g
satisfies the condition in (
19
),
and thus
g=(f_{1}/f_{2})/f_{3}
. A similar argument shows
g=f_{1}/(f_{2}/f_{3})
.

For
k>3
, it is clear that
(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k})|S=((f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1})/f_{k})|S=(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})
,
using (
5
) and induction on
k
. Now take

f^{\prime}
with
f^{\prime}|S=(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})
, and let
j
be the
maximal element of
S
. By (
5
),
f^{\prime}|\{j\}=({}^{j}f,f_{k})

and
{}^{j}f|(S\backslash\{j\})=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k-1})
. So, by inductive
hypothesis,
f^{\prime}\leq{}^{j}f/f_{k}
and
{}^{j}f\leq f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1}
.
Because
/
is order-preserving, this means

f^{\prime}\leq{}^{j}f/f_{k}\leq(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1})/f_{k}=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}.

Hence
f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}=\max\{f\in P_{n}:f|S=(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k})\}
.

That
f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}
is order-preserving is a straightforward
induction on
k
.

To see (
\Delta 4^{\prime}
): if
f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1}/f_{k}=(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{i})/(f_{i+1}/\cdots/f_{k})
,
then clearly
\deg f_{1}+\cdots+\deg f_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, so
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
.
We show the converse by induction on
k
. Given
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
,
let
j
be the maximal element of
S
, so
f={}^{j}f/f_{k}
.
We claim that
S\backslash\{j\}\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}({}^{j}f)
, so, by inductive
hypothesis,
{}^{j}f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1}
, thus
f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k-1}/f_{k}
.

We are left to show that for
1\leq i<j

(20)

i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\quad\iff\quad i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}({}^{j}f)

This is very similar to what we did in (
17
) and (
18
) above but with the basis
M
.
For this we have to establish that the comultiplication on
M
is as in (
6
).
For
f\in P_{n}
and
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
, define

(21)

\Delta_{i}^{\prime}(M_{f}):=\begin{cases}M_{{}^{i}f}\otimes M_{f^{i}}&\text{if }i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f);\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}.

We show now that
\Delta_{+}
agrees with
\sum_{i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)}\Delta_{i}^{\prime}
on the
F
basis:

\displaystyle\Delta_{i}^{\prime}(F_{f})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{i}^{\prime}\big(\sum_{g\geq f}M_{g}\big)=\sum_{g\geq f}\Delta_{i}^{\prime}(M_{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geq f\\
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(g)\end{subarray}}M_{{}^{i}g}\otimes M_{g^{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{}^{i}g\geq^{i}f\\
g^{i}\geq f^{i}\end{subarray}}M_{{}^{i}g}\otimes M_{g^{i}}=F_{{}^{i}f}\otimes F_{f^{i}}

The last equality follows from the fact that for
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(f)
, we have a bijection

\{g:g\geq f\text{ and }i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(g)\}\quad\leftrightarrow\quad\{(^{i}g,g^{i}):{}^{i}g\geq{}^{i}f,g^{i}\geq f^{i}\}.

The forward map given by
g\mapsto({}^{i}g,g^{i})
is well defined by axiom (
\Delta
2), since

g\geq f
implies
{}^{i}g\geq{}^{i}f
and
g^{i}\geq f^{i}
. The reverse map given by
(^{i}g,g^{i})\mapsto{}^{i}g/g^{i}

is well defined by axiom (
\Delta
3), since
{}^{i}g/g^{i}\geq{}^{i}f/f^{i}\geq f
and
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(^{i}g/g^{i})
. It is straightforward to check that those two maps are inverse to each other.
This establishes (
6
) and a proof of (
20
) is similar to the first paragraph of the present proof using the basis
M
instead of
F

in (
17
) and (
18
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We have established the formula for

\Delta_{+}(M_{f})
(
6
) in the final paragraph of the proof of Lemma
7.1
.
To see (
7
), run the proof again with

k
factors instead of 2 factors, using (
\Delta 1^{\prime}
)-(
\Delta 4^{\prime}
) of Lemma
7.1
.
∎

7.2.
Proof of the product formulas for monomial basis

To prove the
k
-factor product formula, and the antipode formula,
we need the following
k
-factor generalizations of axioms (
m
1)-(
m
3):

(
m1^{\prime}
).

For
f_{1}\in C_{j_{1}},f_{2}\in C_{j_{2}},\dots,f_{k}\in C_{j_{k}}
,

(22)

F_{f_{1}}\cdots F_{f_{k}}=\sum_{\zeta\in Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})}F_{\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})},

with
Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})
defined as in (
9
).

(
m2^{\prime}
).

If
f_{i}\leq f^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{j_{i}}
for all
i
, and
\zeta\in Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})
,
then
\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\leq\zeta(f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{k})
.

(
m3^{\prime}
).

For
f_{i},f^{\prime}_{i}\in C_{j_{i}}
for all
i
, and
\zeta\in Sh(C_{j_{1}},\dots,C_{j_{k}})
,
we have

(23)

\zeta(f_{1}\vee f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f_{k}\vee f_{k}^{\prime})\leq\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})\vee\zeta(f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{k}).

Lemma 7.2
.

If
\mathcal{H}

satisfies axioms (
m
0)-(
m
3), then it satisfies (
m1^{\prime}
)-(
m3^{\prime}
).

Proof.

(
m1^{\prime}
) is clear from (
9
) and associativity.
The axiom (
m2^{\prime}
) is proved by induction on
k
, the base case of
k=2
being
(
m
2). To prove (
m3^{\prime}
) by induction on
k
: by (
9
),

\displaystyle(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})(f_{1}\vee f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f_{k}\vee f_{k}^{\prime},)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}(\zeta(f_{1}\vee f^{\prime}_{1},f_{2}\vee f^{\prime}_{2}),f_{3}\vee f^{\prime}_{3},\dots,f_{k}\vee f_{k}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\zeta^{\prime}(\zeta(f_{1},f_{2})\vee\zeta(f_{1},f_{2}),f_{3}\vee f^{\prime}_{3},\dots,f_{k}\vee f_{k}^{\prime}),

using (
m
3) in the first factor, and that
\zeta^{\prime}
is order-preserving
in the first factor by (
m2^{\prime}
). Now apply the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We follow the argument of
[
AS05
, Th. 4.1]
.
Fix
f\in C_{i}\subseteq P_{n}
and
g\in C_{j}\subseteq P_{m}
,
and write
Sh
for the shuffle set
Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
. To multiply

M_{f}
with
M_{g}
, first express them in terms of fundamental
basis elements, then use condition (
m
2) for the product of fundamentals:

\displaystyle M_{f}\cdot M_{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}\mu(f,f^{\prime})\mu(g,g^{\prime})F_{f^{\prime}}\cdot F_{g^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}\>\sum_{\zeta\in Sh}\mu(f,f^{\prime})\mu(g,g^{\prime})F_{\zeta(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{h}\>\sum_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}\>\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\zeta\in Sh,\\
\zeta(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\leq h\end{subarray}}\mu(f,f^{\prime})\mu(g,g^{\prime})M_{h}.

Define

(24)

B_{f,g}^{h}:=\left\{\zeta\in Sh:\zeta(f,g)\leq h\right\},

so that

M_{f}\cdot M_{g}=\sum_{h}\>\sum_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}\mu(f,f^{\prime})\mu(g,g^{\prime})|B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}|M_{h}.

Thus we wish to show

(25)

|A_{f,g}^{h}|=\sum_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}\mu(f,f^{\prime})\mu(g,g^{\prime})|B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}|.

By
Möbius inversion on
C_{i}\times C_{j}
, it suffices to show the disjoint union

(26)

B_{f,g}^{h}=\bigsqcup_{f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g}A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}.

To facilitate the checking of (
26
), we rewrite
conditions (
m
2) and (
m
3) in terms of the
B
sets:

(
m
2*).

if
f^{\prime}\geq f
,
g^{\prime}\geq g
, then
B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f,g}^{h}
;

(
m
3*).

B_{f_{1},g_{1}}^{h}\cap B_{f_{2},g_{2}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2}}^{h}
.

And the definition of
A_{f,g}^{h}
may be written as:

(27)

\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta\in A_{f,g}^{h}\\
\Updownarrow\\
\zeta\in B_{f,g}^{h}\mbox{ and, for all }f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g,\mbox{ with }\zeta\in B_{f,^{\prime}g^{\prime}}^{h},\mbox{ we have }f^{\prime}=f,g^{\prime}=g.\end{array}

The check of (
26
) is done as follows:

•

A_{f_{1},g_{1}}^{h}\cap A_{f_{2},g_{2}}^{h}=\emptyset
.

Indeed, take
\zeta\in A_{f_{1},g_{1}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f_{1},g_{1}}^{h}

and also
\zeta\in A_{f_{2},g_{2}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f_{2},g_{2}}^{h}
.
Then, by (
m
3*),
\zeta\in B_{f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2}}^{h}
.
But, by (
27
),
f_{1}\vee f_{2}=f_{1}
and
g_{1}\vee g_{2}=g_{1}
,
and also
f_{1}\vee f_{2}=f_{2}
and
g_{1}\vee g_{2}=g_{2}
. That is,

f_{1}=f_{2}
and
g_{1}=g_{2}
.

•

If
f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g
, then
A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f,g}^{h}
.

This is because
A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}\subseteq B_{f,g}^{h}
,
by (
m
2*).

•

If
\zeta\in B_{f,g}^{h}
, then
\zeta\in A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}
for some

f^{\prime}\geq f,g^{\prime}\geq g
.

From the maximality condition in the definition
of
A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}
, it’s clear that we need to choose
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
maximal
such that
\zeta\in B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}
- i.e.
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})=\vee\{(f,g):\>\zeta\in B_{f,g}^{h}\}
.
This choice of
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
indeed ensures
\zeta\in B_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}
because
of (
m
3*). And the maximality condition, in order to deduce
\zeta\in A_{f^{\prime},g^{\prime}}^{h}
,
is obvious from the definition of
f^{\prime},g^{\prime}
.

This finishes the proof of (
10
). And the proof of (
11
)
is a
k
-factor version of the above argument, using axioms (
m1^{\prime}
)-(
m3^{\prime}
)
of Lemma
7.2
.
∎

7.3.
Proof of the antipode formula for monomial basis

In this section, fix
f\in P_{n}
and
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. Let
f|S=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})

and let
f_{i}
be in the connected component
C_{i}
.

First, we rewrite
A_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

Suppose conditions (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3)
and (
m
0)-(
m
3) are satisfied. Then, for
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, the set (
11
)
producing the coefficient
\alpha_{f_{1},\dots,f_{k}}^{h}
in Theorem
3.6
may be written as

(28)

A_{f|S}^{h}:=\left\{\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))\middle|\>\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta(f|S)\leq h,\\
\mbox{and if }f^{\prime}\geq f\mbox{ satisfy }\zeta(f^{\prime}|S)\leq h,\\
\mbox{then }f^{\prime}=f.\end{array}\right\}.

Proof.

By comparing with (
11
), it suffices to show that, given

\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))

and
\zeta(f|S)\leq h
, then the following are equivalent:

(i)

If
f_{i}^{\prime}\geq f_{i}
for all
i
and
\zeta(f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{k}^{\prime})\leq h
,
then
f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}
for all
i
;

(ii)

If
f^{\prime}\geq f
satisfy
\zeta(f^{\prime}|S)\leq h
, then
f^{\prime}=f
.

To see (ii)
\implies
(i): given
f_{i}^{\prime}\geq f_{i}
, let
f^{\prime}=f_{1}^{\prime}/f_{2}^{\prime}/\cdots/f_{k}^{\prime}
.
By condition (
\Delta 3^{\prime}
),
/
is order-preserving, so
f^{\prime}\geq f
.
Hence, by (ii),
f^{\prime}=f
; so by (
\Delta 2^{\prime}
),
f^{\prime}|S=f|S
partwise, i.e.

f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}
for all
i
.

To see (i)
\implies
(ii): suppose
f^{\prime}\geq f
, and let
f^{\prime}|S=(f_{1}^{\prime},\dots,f_{k}^{\prime})
.
By (
\Delta 2^{\prime}
),
f_{i}^{\prime}\geq f_{i}
. Hence, by (i),
f_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}

for all
i
. By definition of
/
, we have
f^{\prime}\leq f_{1}^{\prime}/f_{2}^{\prime}/\cdots/f_{k}^{\prime}=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}=f
, where the last equality follows from
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. Hence
f^{\prime}\leq f
,
but by assumption
f^{\prime}\geq f
, so
f^{\prime}=f
.
∎

Following
[
AS05
, Th. 5.5]
, the proof strategy of the antipode
formula is a Möbius inversion, analogous to the proof of the product
formula. By Takeuchi’s formula (details below), the coefficient of

M_{h}
in
\mathcal{S}(M_{f})
will turn out to be
\mu(S,\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f))|A_{f|S}^{h}|
,
where
\mu
is the Möbius function on the Boolean poset of subsets
of
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. Notices that, in this case, the
A
sets are now playing the same role that the
B
sets had in the product formula, and the subsets of
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)

are in the role that the
f
’s had. This suggests doing Möbius inversion
on this Boolean poset, i.e. defining a new set
C_{f|S}^{h}
(analogous
to the
A
’s for the product formula) such that
A_{f|S}^{h}=\amalg_{R\subseteq S}C_{f|R}^{h}
.
However, there is a major obstacle in doing so: the
A
sets are
not nested, meaning
A_{f|S}^{h}\subseteq Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))
and
A_{f|R}^{h}\subseteq Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))\neq Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))
,
so we cannot say
A_{f|R}^{h}\subseteq A_{f|S}^{h}
when
R\subseteq S
.

This problem is not obvious from
[
AS05
, Th. 5.5]
because the set

Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))
is considered as a subset of the same symmetric group, regardless
of
S
. Translating this symmetric group view to the current situation,
we define a set of compatible inclusions

(29)

\iota_{R,S}:Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))\hookrightarrow Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S)),

whenever
R\subseteq S\subseteq GD(f)
, in order to get

(30)

A_{f|S}^{h}=\bigsqcup_{R\subseteq S}\iota_{R,S}(C_{f|R}^{h}).

Lemma 7.4
.

Suppose axioms (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3),
(
m
0)-(
m
3), are all satisfied, and there is a binary associative operation

(f,g)\mapsto f\backslash g
such that, for all connected components
C_{i}

and
C_{j}
, there is some
\zeta_{*}\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
satisfying

f\backslash g=\zeta_{*}(f,g)
, for all
f\in C_{i}
and
g\in C_{j}
.
Further, assume that
f\backslash g\leq f/g
. Then there are compatible
inclusions
\iota_{R,S}:Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))\hookrightarrow Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))

whenever
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. Furthermore, for
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
,
and
\zeta\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))
, we have
(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f|S)\leq\zeta(f|R)
.

Proof.

To set up the inclusion
\iota_{R,S}
, focus on the case where
R

and
S
differ by a single element, since the associativity of the

\backslash
operation means that we can compose such injections to obtain
the general case. So, let
R
consist of all but the
i
th element
of
S
.

Since
f|S=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
and
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, then

f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{i-1}/(f_{i}/f_{i+1})/f_{i+2}/\cdots/f_{k},

by associativity of
/
as in (
\Delta 3^{\prime}
). So
f|R=(f_{1},\dots,f_{i-1},f_{i}/f_{i+1},f_{i+2},\dots,f_{k})
.
Hence

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R)

\displaystyle=(C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(f_{i}/f_{i+1}),C_{i+2},\dots,C_{k})

\displaystyle=(C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(f_{i}\backslash f_{i+1}),C_{i+2},\dots,C_{k}),

because
f_{i}/f_{i+1}>f_{i}\backslash f_{i+1}
. Then, as noted at
the start of Section
3.3
(after Remark (
3.9
)),
we have an injection

Sh(C_{1},\dots,C_{i-1},\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpt}}(\zeta_{*}(C_{i},C_{i+1})),C_{i+2},\dots,C_{k})\hookrightarrow Sh(C_{1},\dots,C_{k}),

from the associativity
in (
9
), where the left-hand side is
Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))
and the right-hand side is
Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))
.

To verify that
(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f|S)\leq\zeta(f|R)
, note that
(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f|S)=\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{i-1},f_{i}\backslash f_{i+1},f_{i+2},\dots,f_{k})
,
and
\zeta(f|R)=\zeta(f_{1},\dots,f_{i-1},f_{i}/f_{i+1},f_{i+2},\dots,f_{k})
.
Now
f_{i}\backslash f_{i+1}<f_{i}/f_{i+1}
, and
\zeta
is order-preserving
by axiom (
m2^{\prime}
).
∎

One more lemma is necessary. We need to convert conditions (
\mathcal{S}
2) and (
\mathcal{S}
3) into
analogues of conditions (
m
2*) and (
m
3*) for the
A
sets instead
of the
B
sets.

Lemma 7.5
.

Suppose conditions (
\Delta
1)-(
\Delta
3),
(
m
0)-(
m
3), (
\mathcal{S}
0)-(
\mathcal{S}
3) are all satisfied. Then,

(
\mathcal{S}
2*).

If
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, then
\iota_{R,S}(A_{f|R}^{h})\subseteq A_{f|S}^{h}
;

(
\mathcal{S}
3*).

For
R_{1},R_{2}\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, we have
\iota_{R_{1},S}(A_{f|R_{1}}^{h})\cap\iota_{R_{2},S}(A_{f|R_{2}}^{h})\subseteq\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(A_{f|R_{1}\cap R_{2}}^{h})
.

Proof.

The argument below is loosely based on the proofs of Claims 1 and
2 within the proof of
[
AS05
, Th. 5.5]
. Following the idea in (
27
), for each statement (
\mathcal{S}
2*) and (
\mathcal{S}
3*),
we separate the definition of
A
’s into two parts, namely a condition on
B
’s
and then the maximality of the
f
’s, so these are the four claims to check:

(
\mathcal{S}
2*a).

If
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, then
\iota_{R,S}(B_{f|R}^{h})\subseteq B_{f|S}^{h}
.

Equivalently,
if
\zeta(f|R)\leq h
, then
(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f|S)\leq h
, which follows from Lemma
7.4
.

(
\mathcal{S}
2*b).

Suppose
R\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
. We have to show:

(31)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{if $\zeta(f|R)\leq h$ and $\zeta(f^{\prime}|R)\leq h$ for some $f^{\prime}\geq f$, then $f^{\prime}=f$,}\\[3.0pt]
\Downarrow\\[3.0pt]
\text{if $\zeta(f|R)\leq h$ and $(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f^{\prime}|S)\leq h$ for some $f^{\prime}\geq f$, then $f^{\prime}=f$.}\end{array}

Let
S=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1}\}
and
R=\{i_{j_{1}},\dots,i_{j_{l}}\}
.
If
f|S
and
f^{\prime}|S
are such that
f|S=(f_{1},\dots,f_{k})
and
f^{\prime}|S=(f^{\prime}_{1},\dots,f^{\prime}_{k})
, we write
f^{\prime}\geq f
if
f^{\prime}_{i}\geq f_{i}
for every
i
. Since
S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f^{\prime})
,
this means
f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{k}
, and
f|R=(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{j_{1}},f_{j_{1}+1}/\cdots/f_{j_{2}},\dots,f_{j_{l-1}+1}/\cdots/f_{j_{l}})
,
and similarly for
f^{\prime}
. Then

\displaystyle\zeta(f|R)

\displaystyle=\zeta(f_{1}/\cdots/f_{j_{1}},f_{j_{1}+1}/\cdots/f_{j_{2}},\dots,f_{j_{l-1}+1}/\cdots/f_{j_{l}});

\displaystyle\iota_{R,S}\zeta(f^{\prime}|S)

\displaystyle=\zeta(f^{\prime}_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{j_{1}},f^{\prime}_{j_{1}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{j_{2}},\dots,f^{\prime}_{j_{l-1}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{j_{l}}).

Using axiom (
m
3), and then axiom (
\mathcal{S}
2) to each input, together
with axiom (
m
2) (that
\zeta
is order-preserving), we have:

\displaystyle\zeta(f|R)\vee\iota_{R,S}\zeta(f^{\prime}|S)

\displaystyle\geq\zeta((f_{1}/\cdots/f_{j_{1}})\vee(f^{\prime}_{1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{j_{1}}),\dots,(f_{j_{l-1}+1}/\cdots/f_{j_{l}})\vee(f^{\prime}_{j_{l-1}+1}\backslash\cdots\backslash f^{\prime}_{j_{l}}))

\displaystyle\geq\zeta(f^{\prime}_{1}/\cdots/f^{\prime}_{j_{1}},\dots,f^{\prime}_{j_{l-1}+1}/\cdots/f^{\prime}_{j_{l}})

\displaystyle=\zeta(f^{\prime}|R).

Thus, if
\zeta(f|R)\leq h
and
(\iota_{R,S}\zeta)(f^{\prime}|S)\leq h
,
we must have
\zeta(f^{\prime}|R)\leq h
. So, using the hypothesis of (
31
), we conclude that
f^{\prime}=f
.

(
\mathcal{S}
3*a).

We will show that

\iota_{R_{1},S}(B_{f|R_{1}}^{h})\cap\iota_{R_{2},S}(B_{f|R_{2}}^{h})\subseteq\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(B_{f|R_{1}\cap R_{2}}^{h}),

i.e. if
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1},S}(\zeta_{1})=\iota_{R_{2},S}(\zeta_{2})

satisfies
\zeta_{1}(f|R_{1})\leq h
and
\zeta_{2}(f|R_{2})\leq h
,
then
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(\zeta^{\prime})
and
\zeta^{\prime}(f|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq h
.

The claim
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(\zeta^{\prime})
is given by (
\mathcal{S}
0).
We note here that
\zeta_{1}=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{1}}(\zeta^{\prime})

and
\zeta_{2}=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{2}}(\zeta^{\prime})
, since

\iota_{R_{1},S}(\zeta_{1})=\zeta=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(\zeta^{\prime})=\iota_{R_{1},S}\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{1}}(\zeta^{\prime}),

and the fact that
\iota_{R_{1},S}
is injective.

We explain with an example how condition (
\mathcal{S}
3)
implies

\zeta^{\prime}(f|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq\zeta_{1}(f|R_{1})\vee\zeta_{2}(f|R_{2})

(and thus
\zeta^{\prime}(f|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq h
as required). Suppose

S=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}\}
,
R_{1}=\{i_{1},i_{3,}i_{4}\}
,
R_{2}=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{6}\}
,
and let
f|S=(f_{1},\dots,f_{7})
. Then
f=f_{1}/\cdots/f_{7}
and

\displaystyle f|R_{1}\cap R_{2}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},f_{2}/f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7});

\displaystyle f|R_{1}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},f_{2}/f_{3},f_{4},f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7});

\displaystyle f|R_{2}

\displaystyle=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6},f_{7}).

This implies:

\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}(f|R_{1}\cap R_{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1},f_{2}/f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7});

(32)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(f|R_{1})=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{1}}(f|R_{1})

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1},(f_{2}/f_{3})\backslash f_{4}\backslash(f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7}));

(33)

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}(f|R_{2})=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{2}}(f|R_{2})

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1},f_{2}\backslash(f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6})\backslash f_{7}).

Examine the second input of
\zeta^{\prime}
in (
32
) and
(
33
). First note that
f_{5}
and
f_{6}
appear
only as
f_{5}/f_{6}
(because
i_{5}\notin R_{1}\cup R_{2}
), so
regard
f_{5}/f_{6}
as one object, instead of
f_{5}
and
f_{6}

independently. Next, because
i_{2},i_{3},i_{4,}i_{6}\in(R_{1}\cup R_{2})\backslash(R_{1}\cap R_{2})
,
there is a
/
sign in one of the identities (
32
) and (
33
), and a
\backslash
sign in the same position of remaining identity (between
f_{2}
and
f_{3}
; between
f_{3}
and
f_{4}
; between
f_{4}
and
f_{5}/f_{6}
, and
between
f_{5}/f_{6}
and
f_{7}
). Thus condition

S
3 applies to give

f_{2}/f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7}\leq(f_{2}/f_{3})\backslash f_{4}\backslash(f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7})\vee f_{2}\backslash(f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6})\backslash f_{7}.

(In the general case, we need one such equation for each input position
where the inputs of
\zeta^{\prime}
in (
32
) and (
33
)
disagree.) So, by conditions (
m
2) and (
m
3):

\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1}/f_{2}/f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7})\leq\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1},(f_{2}/f_{3})\backslash f_{4}\backslash(f_{5}/f_{6}/f_{7}))\vee\zeta^{\prime}(f_{1},f_{2}\backslash(f_{3}/f_{4}/f_{5}/f_{6})\backslash f_{7}).

(
\mathcal{S}
3*b).

Suppose
\zeta,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta^{\prime}
are as above. We will show that

(34)

\begin{array}[]{c}\text{if $\zeta_{1}(f^{\prime}|R_{1})\leq h$ and $\zeta_{2}(f^{\prime}|R_{2})\leq h$ for some $f^{\prime}\geq f$, then $f^{\prime}=f$,}\\[3.0pt]
\Downarrow\\[3.0pt]
\text{if $\zeta^{\prime}(f^{\prime}|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq h$ for some $f^{\prime}\geq f$, then $f^{\prime}=f$.}\end{array}

Assume
\zeta^{\prime}(f^{\prime}|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq h
. By Lemma
7.4
,

R_{1}\cap R_{2}\subseteq R_{1}
means
\zeta_{1}(f^{\prime}|R_{1})\leq\zeta^{\prime}(f^{\prime}|R_{1}\cap R_{2})\leq h
,
and similarly
\zeta_{2}(f^{\prime}|R_{2})\leq h
. So, by the hypothesis of (
34
),

f^{\prime}=f
.

∎

Now we are finally ready to prove the antipode formula.

Proof of Theorem
3.10
.

Takeuchi’s formula says

\mathcal{S}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\deg x}(-1)^{k+1}(m\otimes\cdots\otimes m)\circ\Delta_{+}^{[k]}(x).

Using (
7
) and Lemma
7.3
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(M_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}(-1)^{|S|+1}\sum_{h}|A_{f|S}^{h}|M_{h}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)|+1}\sum_{h}\sum_{S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}\mu(S,\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f))|A_{f|S}^{h}|M_{h},

where
\mu
is relative to the Boolean poset of subsets of
\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
.
Now interpret
{\displaystyle\sum_{S\subseteq T}\mu(S,T)|A_{f|S}^{h}|}

by Möbius inversion on the Boolean poset with
A_{f|S}^{h}
. That is,
we partition
A_{f|S}^{h}
according to where
S
is minimal - hence
the third condition in (
36
) compared to (
28
).
More specifically, we show that

(35)

A_{f|S}^{h}=\bigsqcup_{R\subseteq S}\iota_{R,S}(C_{f|R}^{h}),

where
C_{f|S}^{h}
are the shuffles in
Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|S))
satisfying

(36)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{(i)}&\zeta(f|S)\leq h;\\
\text{(ii)}&\mbox{if }f^{\prime}\geq f\mbox{ satisfies }\zeta(f^{\prime}|S)\leq h,\mbox{ then }f=f^{\prime};\\
\text{(iii)}&\mbox{if }\zeta=\iota_{R,S}\zeta^{\prime}\text{ for some }R\subseteq S\text{ and some }\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}f|R),\\
&\text{ and }\zeta^{\prime}(f|R)\leq h\mbox{, then }R=S.\end{array}

Observe that the definition of
C_{f|S}^{h}
relative to
A_{f|S}^{h}

is not exactly like the definition of
B_{f,g}^{h}
relative to
A_{f,g}^{h}
:
the third condition in the definition of
C_{f|S}^{h}
only specifies
the minimality of
S
for
\zeta(f|S)\leq h
, not also for the second
line regarding the maximality of
f
. In other words,

(37)

\begin{array}[]{c}\zeta\in C_{f|S}^{h}\\
\Updownarrow\\
\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}\mbox{ and, if }\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})\text{ for some }R\subseteq S\text{ with }\zeta\in B_{f|R}^{h},\mbox{ we have }R=S.\end{array}

To prove the partitioning analogous to the proof of the product formula
(Theorem
3.6
), we need to change the
B_{f|R}^{h}

to
A_{f|R}^{h}
in this definition of
C_{f|S}^{h}
. So we need
to prove that the following are equivalent:

(i)

\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}
and, if
\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})
for
some
R\subseteq S
with
\zeta^{\prime}\in B_{f|R}^{h}
, we have
R=S
.

(ii)

\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}
and, if
\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})
for some

R\subseteq S
with
\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f|R}^{h}
, we have
R=S
.

Since
B_{f|R}^{h}\supseteq A_{f|R}^{h}
, (i)
\implies
(ii) is immediate.
To show (ii)
\implies
(i): since the “conclusion” for (i) and (ii) are
equal (“we have
R=S
”), it suffices to show that the hypotheses
for (i) imply the hypotheses for (ii).

Recall
B_{f|R}^{h}=\amalg_{f^{\prime}\geq f}A_{f^{\prime}|R}^{h}
.
So we show

\mbox{if }\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}\mbox{, and }\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})\text{ for some }R\subseteq S\mbox{ and }\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f^{\prime}|R}^{h}\mbox{ for some }f^{\prime}\geq f,\mbox{ then }\zeta\in A_{f|R}^{h}.

Here’s the proof:
\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f^{\prime}|R}^{h}
and
\iota_{R,S}(A_{f^{\prime}|R}^{h})\subseteq A_{f^{\prime}|S}^{h}

by condition (
\mathcal{S}
2*). So
\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}
and
\zeta\in A_{f^{\prime}|S}^{h}
.
By the maximality of
f
in the definition of
A
(
28
),
this implies
f=f^{\prime}
. So
\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f^{\prime}|R}^{h}
actually means

\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f|R}^{h}
.

Having established this equivalent and more useful definition of
C_{f|S}^{h}
,
we can continue the proof of the antipode formula in exactly the same
manner as for the product formula.

•

For any
R_{1},R_{2}\subseteq S\subseteq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
, we have the empty intersection

\iota_{R_{1},S}(C_{f|R_{1}}^{h})\cap\iota_{R_{2},S}(C_{f|R_{2}}^{h})=\emptyset
.

Indeed, take
\zeta\in\iota_{R_{1},S}(C_{f|R_{1}}^{h})\cap\iota_{R_{2},S}(C_{f|R_{2}}^{h})
,
so
\zeta=\iota_{R_{1},S}(\zeta_{1})=\iota_{R_{2},S}(\zeta_{2})

with
\zeta\in C_{f|S}^{h}\subseteq A_{f|S}^{h}
. By condition
(
\mathcal{S}
3*), there is some
\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f|R_{1}\cap R_{2}}^{h}
with

\zeta=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},S}(\zeta^{\prime})
, and recall from the proof
of (
\mathcal{S}
3*a) within Lemma
7.5
that
\zeta_{1}=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{1}}(\zeta^{\prime})

and
\zeta_{2}=\iota_{R_{1}\cap R_{2},R_{2}}(\zeta^{\prime})
. As
\zeta_{1}\in C_{f|R_{1}}^{h}
,
the minimality condition means
R_{1}\cap R_{2}=R_{1}
, and similarly

R_{1}\cap R_{2}=R_{2}
. So
R_{1}=R_{2}
.

•

if
R\subseteq S
, then
\iota_{R,S}(C_{f|R}^{h})\subseteq A_{f|S}^{h}
.

This is because
C_{f|R}^{h}\subseteq A_{f|R}^{h}
and
\iota_{R,S}(A_{f|S}^{h})\subseteq A_{f|S}^{h}
,
by condition (
\mathcal{S}
2*).

•

if
\zeta\in A_{f|S}^{h}
, then
\zeta\in\iota_{R,S}(C_{f|R}^{h})

for some
R\subseteq S
.

From the minimality condition in the definition of
C_{f|R}^{h}
,
it’s clear that we need to choose
R
minimal such that
\zeta\in\iota_{R,S}(C_{f|R}^{h})

- i.e.
R=\cap\{R^{\prime}:\>\zeta\in\iota_{R^{\prime},S}(C_{f|R^{\prime}}^{h})\}
. This
choice of
R
indeed ensures
\zeta\in\iota_{R,S}(A_{f|R}^{h})

because of condition (
\mathcal{S}
3*). So
\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})
for
some
\zeta^{\prime}\in A_{f|R}^{h}
. To deduce that
\zeta^{\prime}\in C_{f|R}^{h}

(i.e. to see the minimality of
R
), suppose
\zeta^{\prime}=\iota_{R^{\prime},R}(\zeta^{\prime\prime})

for some
R^{\prime}\subseteq R
and some
\zeta^{\prime\prime}\in A_{f|R^{\prime}}^{h}
. Then

\zeta=\iota_{R,S}(\zeta^{\prime})=\iota_{R,S}\iota_{R^{\prime},R}(\zeta^{\prime\prime})=\iota_{R^{\prime},S}(\zeta^{\prime\prime}),

so
R\subseteq R^{\prime}
by definition of
R
. Hence
R^{\prime}=R
as required.

Finally, to establish that
\beta_{f}^{h}=|C_{f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}^{h}|
, we show the equivalence of the last condition in the definition
of
C_{f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}^{h}
in (
36
) with the last condition in (
12
).
Clearly, the last condition in (
36
) is equivalent to the following:
if
\zeta=\iota_{R,\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)}\zeta^{\prime}
for some
R\subsetneq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
and
some
\zeta^{\prime}\in Sh(\operatorname{\mathtt{cmpts}}(f|R))
, then
\zeta^{\prime}(f|R)\not\leq h
. The statement in (
12
)
says that it is sufficient to check this condition when
R=\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\}
,
for some
i
. The reason is that, by Lemma
7.4
,

(\iota_{R,\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\}}\zeta^{\prime})(f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\})\leq\zeta^{\prime}(f|R)
,
for any
R\subsetneq\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)
and
i\in\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash R
. So, if
\zeta^{\prime}(f|R)\leq h
,
then necessarily
(\iota_{R,\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\}}\zeta^{\prime})(f|\operatorname{\mathtt{GDes}}(f)\backslash\{i\})\leq h
.
∎

7.4.
Proof of the inheritance of axioms through quotienting

Proof of Theorem
3.16
.

Let
\Pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\bar{\mathcal{H}}
be defined by
(
14
). Then, for
f\in P_{n}
:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}(F_{f})

\displaystyle=\sum_{g\geq f}\Pi^{\prime}(M_{g})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\iota_{n}(t)\geq f}M_{t}.

We claim this equals
\Pi(F_{f})=F_{\pi_{n}(f)}=\sum_{t\geq\pi_{n}(f)}M_{t}
.
It suffices to show that
\iota_{n}(t)\geq f
if and only if
t\geq\pi_{n}(f)
, i.e.
\pi_{n}
and
\iota_{n}
form a Galois connection, which can be proved similar to Corollary
5.27
.

In the first scenario under “Furthermore”: it follows from (
\pi\Delta
1),
(
\pi\Delta
2) and (
\Delta
1) on
\mathcal{H}
that
\Pi
is a coalgebra
morphism. To check
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies (
\Delta
1): if
s\leq t
,
then
\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(s)=\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(\iota_{n}(s))=\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(\iota_{n}(t))=\operatorname{\mathtt{allow}}(t)
,
where the first equality is by (
\pi\Delta
1), because
s=\pi_{n}(\iota_{n}s)
,
and the middle equality is because
\iota_{n}(s)\leq\iota_{n}(t)
.

To check
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies (
\Delta
3):
given
s\in\bar{P}_{i},t\in\bar{P}_{n-i}
, first note that

{}^{i}(s/t)={}^{i}\pi_{n}(\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t))=\pi_{n}[{}^{i}(\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t))],

by definition of
/
on
\bar{P}
and by (
\pi\Delta
2). By definition
of
/
on
P
,
\pi_{n}[{}^{i}(\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t))]=\pi_{i}\iota_{i}(s)=s
,
and similarly
(s/t)^{i}=t
. To check the maximality, suppose
r\in\bar{P}_{n}

satisfies
{}^{i}r=s
and
r^{i}=t
, and let
f=\iota_{n}(r)
.
Then
\pi_{i}({}^{i}f)={}^{i}\pi_{n}(f)={}^{i}r=s
, so the definition
of
\iota_{i}
implies
{}^{i}f\leq\iota_{i}(s)
. Similarly,
f^{i}\leq\iota_{n-i}(t)
.
So
f\leq{}^{i}f/f^{i}\leq\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t)
. And
\pi_{n}

is order-preserving, so
r=\pi_{n}(f)\leq\pi_{n}(\iota_{i}(s)/\iota_{n-i}(t))=s/t

by definition. That
/
is order-preserving on
\bar{P}
, and axiom
(
\Delta
2), can be proved similarly.

Similarly, in the second scenario, it follows from (
\pi m
1), (
\pi m
2)
and (
m
1) on
\mathcal{H}
that
\Pi
is an algebra morphism. Axiom
(
m
0) holds for
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
by Lemma
3.15
.
(
m
1) holds for
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
because of (
\pi m
1). Axiom
(
m
2) can be checked in a similar way as (
\Delta
3) above. To deduce
(
m
3): given
s_{1},s_{2}\in\bar{C}_{i}\subseteq\bar{P}_{n}
,
t_{1},t_{2}\in\bar{C}_{j}\subseteq\bar{P}_{m}
,
consider
f_{l}=\iota_{n}(s_{l})
,
g_{l}=\iota_{m}(t_{l})
for

l=1,2
. Take
\bar{\zeta}\in Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})
corresponding
to
\zeta\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
. Then

\bar{\zeta}(s_{1},t_{1})=\bar{\zeta}(\pi_{n}(f_{1}),\pi_{m}(g_{1}))=\pi_{n+m}\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})

and similarly for
s_{2},t_{2}
,
so

\bar{\zeta}(s_{1},t_{1})\vee\bar{\zeta}(s_{2},t_{2})=\pi_{n+m}\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\pi_{n+m}\zeta(f_{2},g_{2})=\pi_{n+m}(\zeta(f_{1},g_{1})\vee\zeta(f_{2},g_{2}))

by Lemma
3.15
. Because (
m
3) holds
on
\mathcal{H}
and
\pi_{n+m}
is order-preserving, this is
\geq\pi_{n+m}(\zeta(f_{1}\vee f_{2},g_{1}\vee g_{2}))=\bar{\zeta}(\pi_{n+m}(f_{1}\vee f_{2}),\pi_{n+m}(g_{1}\vee g_{2}))=\bar{\zeta}(s_{1}\vee t_{1},s_{2}\vee t_{2})
,
using Lemma
3.15
again in the last
equality.

In the third scenario: to show (
\mathcal{S}
0) on
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
,
let
\backslash\in Sh(\bar{C}_{i},\bar{C}_{j})
be the shuffle corresponding
to
\backslash\in Sh(C_{i},C_{j})
. By (
\pi m
2), we have
\pi_{n}(g\backslash h)=\pi_{i}(g)\backslash\pi_{n-i}(h)
,
so in particular
s\backslash t=\pi_{n}(\iota_{i}(s)\backslash\iota_{n-i}(t))
.
The requirement
s\backslash t\leq s/t
, and (
\mathcal{S}
2) and
(
\mathcal{S}
3), can be checked for
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
by lifting
to
\mathcal{H}
, as for axioms (
\Delta
3) and (
m
3) above. And (
\mathcal{S}
1)
on
\bar{\mathcal{H}}
follows from (
\pi\mathcal{S}
1) and (
\mathcal{S}
1)
on
\mathcal{H}
.
∎

8.
